Quantum Nonlinear Properties from a Single Measurement Setting
Zihao Li, Datong Chen, Dayue Qin, Yuxiang Yang, You Zhou

Abstract
Nonlinear properties of quantum states are essential to quantum information and many-body physics, but assessing them experimentally is challenging, as it typically requires multi-copy operations or a large number of measurement settings. To address this challenge, we develop a universal framework, collision-based nonlinear estimation (CBNE), for efficiently measuring nonlinear quantities of a quantum state ρ \rho , such as the higher-order expectation value tr ⁡ ( O ​ ρ t ) \Tr(O\rho^{t}) for some observable O O , using single-copy randomized measurements. Strikingly, our protocol requires only a single measurement setting, provided that the system dimension is sufficiently large or a few ancillary qubits are available; this contrasts with the conventional expectation that multiple measurement bases are necessary for nonlinear estimation. In addition, CBNE is observable-independent at the experimental stage, which enables simultaneous estimation of multiple nonlinear functions. It further extends to broader tasks, including the estimation of principal component properties and partial-transpose moments of quantum states. Our results provide a practical and scalable route for measuring nonlinear state properties on near-term quantum devices.



Nonlinear functions of quantum systems, such as the state moments
\Tr(\rho^{t})
and higher-order expectation values
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, play central roles in areas ranging from quantum information processing to many-body physics.
They underpin numerous applications including quantum error mitigation

Huggins et al. 2021
;
Koczor 2021
;
Cai et al. 2023
;
Seif et al. 2023
;
Zhou and Liu 2024
;
Hakoshima et al. 2024
;
O’Brien et al. 2023
, quantum virtual cooling (QVC)

Cotler et al. 2019
;
Hakoshima et al. 2024
, entanglement spectroscopy

Johri et al. 2017
;
Yirka and Subaşı 2021
, and quantum entropy estimation
Islam et al. 2015
;
Brydges et al. 2019
;
Elben et al. 2018
;
Vermersch et al. 2018
.
Beyond these, the
partial-transpose
(PT) moments of quantum states
provide powerful criteria for entanglement detection
Gray et al. 2018
;
Elben et al. 2020a
;
Zhou et al. 2020
;
Neven et al. 2021
;
Yu et al. 2021
and serve as useful probes for quantum phases of matter and entanglement dynamics in open systems
Calabrese et al. 2012
;
Ruggiero et al. 2016
;
Wu et al. 2020
.
They also enable access to the entanglement negativity
Plenio 2005
;
Vidal and Werner 2002
;
Carrasco et al. 2024
;
Carteret 2016
, a prominent entanglement measure for mixed quantum states.

Conventional approaches for accessing these nonlinear quantities, such as generalized swap tests
Ekert et al. 2002
;
Bruni 2004
;
Quek et al. 2024
, require collective operations on multiple copies of the state prepared in parallel
Liu and Wang 2025
;
Liu et al. 2026
;
Shin et al. 2025
;
Carteret 2005
;
Liu et al. 2022
;
Zhang et al. 2026
;
Chen et al. 2025
;
Shi et al. 2025
;
Ye et al. 2025
, which are experimentally demanding.
More recently,
randomized measurement
(RM) protocols and the framework of
classical shadow estimation
(CSE) have emerged as powerful single-copy alternatives
van Enk and Beenakker 2012
;
Huang et al. 2020
;
Vermersch et al. 2024a
;
Elben et al. 2020b
;
Elben et al. 2023
;
Elben et al. 2019
;
van Enk and Beenakker 2012
;
Hu et al. 2023
;
Vermersch et al. 2024b
;
Arienzo et al. 2023
;
Ippoliti et al. 2023
.
By applying random circuits followed by fixed-basis measurements, these methods enable the estimation of nonlinear properties from the classical correlations between independent measurement runs
Elben et al. 2023
.

However, the implementation of these single-copy methods still incurs a substantial overhead in the number of random circuits (i.e., measurement settings) used. Specifically, to achieve an estimation error
\epsilon
, the required setting number usually scales as
\sim 1/\epsilon^{2}
and, for many tasks, can even grow exponentially with the system size
Huang et al. 2020
;
Rath et al. 2021
;
Elben et al. 2020a
;
Pelecanos et al. 2026
;
Elben et al. 2023
.
This poses a significant practical bottleneck for near-term devices, as frequent switching between measurement settings is resource-intensive, and most hardware platforms prefer to operate
with minimal setting variations
Helsen and Walter 2023
;
Zhou and Liu 2023
;
Struchalin et al. 2021
;
Huggins et al. 2022
;
Zhou et al. 2020
.
Unfortunately, estimating nonlinear functions with a constant number of single-copy measurement settings is currently achievable only for second-order quantities, such as
\Tr(\rho^{2})
and
\Tr(O\rho^{2})
with specific structured observables
O

Elben et al. 2020b
;
Anshu et al. 2022
;
Du et al. 2026
.

Figure 1:
Schematic illustration of the CBNE framework.
Left: experimental stage. A fixed random unitary is repeatedly applied to sequentially prepared copies of
\rho
, followed by projective measurements in the computational basis.
Right: classical postprocessing. Collision estimators constructed from the measurement outcomes enable the estimation of nonlinear functions of
\rho
, including
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
,
\Tr(\rho^{t})
, principal component properties, PT moments, etc.

In this work, we overcome this limitation by introducing a universal and observable-independent framework, termed
collision-based nonlinear estimation
(CBNE), for measuring nonlinear functions such as
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, including
\Tr(\rho^{t})
, from single-copy RMs.
As illustrated in Fig.
1
, CBNE requires only
a single
fixed random unitary followed by projective measurements, provided that either the system dimension is sufficiently large or a small number,
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\log\epsilon^{-1}}}\right)
, of ancillary qubits are accessible.
This substantially reduces the experimental overhead compared to existing approaches (see Table
1
in the End Matter), suggesting that the conventional reliance on many measurement bases is not intrinsic.
Inheriting the mindset of CSE, CBNE enables the estimation of multiple nonlinear functions from the same measurement data through simple and efficient linear postprocessing based on collision statistics.
Moreover, it achieves the best-known sample cost for general orders
t\geq 3
among existing single-copy measurement protocols.
Beyond these, CBNE can be applied to learn principal component properties of quantum states, and admits a natural generalization to measuring PT moments, thereby enabling entanglement detection with a single measurement setting.

Collision-based estimation from RMs
—
We first present the basic version of the CBNE protocol, leaving its variants and extensions to later sections.
Consider an unknown
n
-qubit quantum state
\rho
on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
that can be prepared repeatedly. As shown in Fig.
1
, the experimental stage of CBNE builds upon RMs
Elben et al. 2023
and proceeds as follows.
We apply to
\rho
a random unitary
U
drawn from some ensemble
\mathcal{E}
, then measure the state in the computational basis
\{|b\rangle\}_{b=0}^{d-1}
, where
d=2^{n}
is the system dimension.
This process is repeated for
N_{U}
distinct random unitaries, with
N_{M}
projective measurements performed for each unitary.

To illustrate the key idea of CBNE, let us first consider the estimation of state moments
p_{t}=\Tr(\rho^{t})
.
Let
\mathbf{b}_{U}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}\in[d]^{N_{M}}
be the list of measurement outcomes collected under a fixed random unitary
U
.
In postprocessing, we calculate the following estimator by counting the number of
k
-wise collisions among the elements in
\mathbf{b}_{U}
(for each
k=2,\dots,t
):

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{k}^{U}:=\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d\binom{N_{M}}{k}}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}}\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\},

(1)

where
\textbf{1}\{Y\}=1
if the event
Y
is true and 0 otherwise.
As shown in Supplementary Material (SM) Sec.
S6
,
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
can be efficiently computed classically.
In addition, when the ensemble
\mathcal{E}
forms a unitary
t
-design
Mele 2024
, its expectation value (taken over the randomness in unitary selection and in quantum measurements) is given by (see SM Sec.
S2
):

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]\!=\kappa_{k}\,\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\Pr}_{\rho}(b|U)^{k}}}\right]=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\!\Tr(\rho^{\otimes k}R_{\pi}):=\zeta_{k},

(2)

where
\Pr_{\rho}(b|U):=\langle b|U\rho U^{\dagger}|b\rangle
,
\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[\cdot]
denotes the average over random unitaries in
\mathcal{E}
,
\kappa_{k}=\binom{k+d-1}{k}
,

\mathrm{S}_{k}
denotes the permutation group of order
k
,
and
R_{\pi}
is the permutation operator on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes k}

associated with
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}
.

In the summation of Eq. (
2
), each
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}
contributes according to its cycle structure, making
\zeta_{k}
a polynomial in
\{p_{j}\}_{j\leq k}

van Enk and Beenakker 2012
:

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{p_{2}}{2},\quad\zeta_{3}=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{p_{2}}{3}+\frac{p_{3}}{2},\quad\dots

(3)

Let
\hat{\zeta}_{k}:=\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{M}_{k}^{U_{s}}/N_{U}
denote the estimator of
\zeta_{k}
obtained by averaging over
N_{U}
random unitaries.
Finally, by substituting these estimates into Eq. (
3
), we can sequentially solve for
p_{2},\dots,p_{t}
.

We note that state moment estimation via the relation (
2
) was previously suggested by Ref.
van Enk and Beenakker 2012
.
However, our CBNE protocol is conceptually different:
Instead of estimating individual outcome probabilities
\Pr_{\rho}(b|U)
,
we directly measure their power sums through collision statistics, thereby significantly reducing the resource cost; see SM Sec.
S7
for details.

From moments to more general nonlinear functions
—
The CBNE framework extends naturally from moments
p_{t}
to
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
.
Let
O_{0}:=O-\Tr(O)\openone/d
be the traceless part of
O
.
Using the data
\mathbf{b}_{U}
, we introduce the following estimator by generalizing
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
in Eq. (
1
):

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0}):=\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d\binom{N_{M}}{k}}\sum_{i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}}\!\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}\!=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\}\,\widetilde{\Pr}_{O_{0}}(\hat{b}_{i_{1}}|U).

(4)

Here
\widetilde{\Pr}_{O_{0}}(b|U)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|UO_{0}U^{{\dagger}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle
represents the
quasi
-outcome probability of (fictitious) computational-basis measurements on the
pseudostate

UO_{0}U^{{\dagger}}
, and can be computed classically.

When the ensemble
\mathcal{E}
forms a unitary
(t+1)
-design,

\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})
is an unbiased estimator for (see SM Sec.
S3
):

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})}}\right]=\frac{\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k+1}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}\otimes O_{0}}}\right)R_{\pi}}}\right]}{(k+1)!}:=\xi_{k}^{O_{0}}.

(5)

By definition,
\xi_{k}^{O_{0}}
can be expanded as a polynomial in

p_{j}
and
\Tr(O_{0}\rho^{i})
with
1\leq i,j\leq k
:

\displaystyle\!\xi_{1}^{O_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr(O_{0}\rho)}{2},\quad\ \xi_{2}^{O_{0}}=\frac{\Tr(O_{0}\rho)+\Tr(O_{0}\rho^{2})}{3},

\displaystyle\!\xi_{3}^{O_{0}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+p_{2})\Tr(O_{0}\rho)}{8}+\frac{\Tr(O_{0}\rho^{2})+\Tr(O_{0}\rho^{3})}{4},\ \,\dots

(6)

Inverting these relations using the estimated values of
\xi_{i}^{O_{0}}
and
p_{j}
yields
\Tr(O_{0}\rho^{k})
sequentially for
k=1,2,\dots,t
.
Consequently, we obtain
\Tr(O\rho^{k})=\Tr(O_{0}\rho^{k})+\Tr(O)p_{k}/d
.

The complete CBNE protocol is summarized in Algorithm
1
in the End Matter. Its performance is guaranteed by the following theorem; see SM Sec.
S3
for a proof.

Theorem 1
.

Suppose
t\geq 1
is a constant integer and
O
is an observable with bounded operator norm
\|O\|\leq 1
.
Let
O_{0}:=O-\Tr(O)\openone/d
and
\mathfrak{B}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Tr(O_{0}^{2}),1}}\right\}
.
For any
0<\epsilon<1
, the CBNE protocol can return
\epsilon
-additive error estimates for all
\Tr(O\rho),\dots,\Tr(O\rho^{t})
and
p_{2},\dots,p_{t}
with high probability, provided that

\displaystyle N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,c_{1}\,\Xi_{1}}}\right\},\ \ N_{M}\geq c_{2}\,d\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Xi_{1}^{1/t},1}}\right\},

(7)

and the ensemble
\mathcal{E}
forms a unitary
2(t+1)
-design.
Here,
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are some constants independent of
\mathfrak{B},d
and
\epsilon
, and
\Xi_{1}
is a shorthand for
\Xi_{1}(d,\mathfrak{B},\epsilon):=\mathfrak{B}/(d\epsilon^{2})
.
State moment estimation is recovered by taking
O=\openone
.

Our CBNE protocol exhibits notable advantages in several aspects
(see Table
1
in the End Matter and SM Sec.
S7
for comparisons with existing approaches).

First, from an experimental perspective, it requires only a single measurement setting (
N_{U}=1
) when the system dimension is sufficiently large, i.e.,
d\geq c_{1}\mathfrak{B}/\epsilon^{2}
, a condition that is generically satisfied for
O=\openone
and for observables with bounded rank.
If instead
d<c_{1}\mathfrak{B}/\epsilon^{2}
, single-setting estimation can still be achieved via a slight modification of the basic framework:
after preparing each
\rho
, we additionally introduce

\displaystyle n_{a}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\log_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{c_{1}\mathfrak{B}}{d\,\epsilon^{2}}}}\right)}}\right\rceil\leq 2\log_{2}(\epsilon^{-1})+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)

(8)

ancillary qubits (initialized in the trivial state
\rho_{a}=|0^{n_{a}}\rangle\langle 0^{n_{a}}|
) to increase the system size;
the CBNE protocol is then executed within the extended system
\tilde{\rho}=\rho\otimes\rho_{a}
and observable
\tilde{O}=O\otimes\rho_{a}
, preserving the target value
\Tr(\tilde{O}\tilde{\rho}^{\,t})=\Tr(O\rho^{t})
.
This modification reduces the number of required measurement settings to
N_{U}=1
, while preserving the overall sample cost
eff
.

Second, the sample complexity of our protocol reads

\displaystyle N_{\text{tot}}=N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\bigg\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}\mathfrak{B}^{1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},\frac{\mathfrak{B}}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg\}}}\right),

(9)

which is the best known result so far for order
t\geq 3
.
In particular, in the high-precision regime
\epsilon^{2}\leq\mathfrak{B}/d
,
the cost becomes
N_{\text{tot}}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathfrak{B}/\epsilon^{2}}}\right)
, which achieves the same scaling as the CSE method for estimating
\Tr(O\rho)

Huang et al. 2020
, demonstrating that nonlinear functions can be estimated as efficiently as linear ones in this regime.

Third, our protocol inherits and further generalizes two key advantages of CSE
Huang et al. 2020
:
(i) The experimental stage is independent of the target observable, enabling simultaneous estimation of multiple observables;
(ii) As analyzed in SM Sec.
S6
, the classical postprocessing overhead of CBNE is minimal, reducing to simple collision counting with few or no matrix operations, and is even much lower than that of CSE.

Fourth, as shown in the End Matter,
when the target observable
O
has a low rank or equals the identity
\openone
,
log-depth random circuits drawn from an approximate unitary design
Schuster et al. 2025
;
Cui et al. 2025
;
Cui et al. 2025

can be applied in place of the random unitaries used in CBNE,
while retaining almost the same performance guarantees.
This further drastically reduces the experimental overhead,
making the protocol appealing to near-term quantum devices
Preskill 2018
.

Numerical results
—
We validate the performance of CBNE by numerically simulating the estimation of
p_{3}=\Tr(\rho^{3})
. The target state
\rho
is chosen as the ground state of the 1D
transverse-field Ising model
(TFIM) with open boundary conditions, whose Hamiltonian has the form

H=-J\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}X_{i}X_{i+1}-h\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}
.
For computational feasibility, the unitary ensemble used in our simulation is generated via
2
-local brickwork circuits composed of nearest-neighbor Haar-random gates, which are known to form an approximate design
Brandão et al. 2016
;
Haferkamp 2022
.

Figure 2:
Average statistical error in estimating
p_{3}
via CBNE. Here
\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+pI/d
,
p=0.2
, and
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
is the ground state of TFIM with
J=h=1.0
.
(a) Error scaling versus
N_{M}
for
N_{U}=1
across different system sizes
n
; the dashed black line represents the scaling
\propto N_{M}^{-3/2}
.
(b) Error scaling versus
n
for fixed
N_{M}=10^{8}
and different
N_{U}
.

We investigate the scaling behavior of the estimation error with respect to the system size
n
and the number of measurements
N_{M}
per unitary.
Figure
2
(a) reveals two distinct scaling regimes.
In the small
N_{M}
regime, the error is proportional to
N_{M}^{-3/2}
, and decreases rapidly with increasing
N_{M}
.
This behavior is consistent with our theoretical result in Theorem
1
, which shows that the error in estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
scales as
N_{M}^{-t/2}
when
N_{M}\ll d
.
In the large
N_{M}
regime, the error saturates and instead
decreases exponentially with
n
, as illustrated in Fig.
2
(b).
This exponential suppression agrees with the theoretical prediction in Theorem
1
,
demonstrating that moment estimation can be achieved with a single measurement setting when the system size is large enough.

Principal component estimation
—
To further showcase the versatility of CBNE, we apply it to the task of
principal component estimation
(PCE),
which is valuable for many applications such as error mitigation

Huggins et al. 2021
;
Koczor 2021
;
Cai et al. 2023
;
Seif et al. 2023
;
O’Brien et al. 2023
;
Hakoshima et al. 2024
.
In this task, we are given copies of an unknown state
\rho
,
and the goal is to estimate eigen-property
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi}}\right|O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle
of the principal component of
\rho
.
Here
O
is an arbitrary observable with
\|O\|\leq 1
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle
is the principal eigenstate of
\rho
that corresponds to the largest eigenvalue
\lambda
.
Following prior works
Huang et al. 2022
;
Grier et al. 2024
, we assume that

\lambda
is larger than all other eigenvalues of
\rho
by a constant that is independent of the system dimension
d
.

To address the PCE problem, recent near-term proposals
Huggins et al. 2021
;
Koczor 2021
;
Hakoshima et al. 2024
consider the estimation of
\Tr(O\rho^{t})/p_{t}
, which converges to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi}}\right|O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle
as
t
increases.
Our CBNE protocol directly estimates both
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
and
p_{t}
, and thus provides a natural approach to PCE.
Its performance guarantees for this task are summarized below; see SM Sec.
S4
for a proof.

Theorem 2
.

The CBNE protocol can estimate
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi}}\right|O\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle
up to any constant additive error with high probability,
provided that
t\geq c_{1}
,
N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,c_{2}\mathfrak{B}/d}}\right\}
,
N_{M}\geq c_{3}d
.
Here
\mathfrak{B}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Tr(O_{0}^{2}),1}}\right\}
, and
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
are some constants independent of
\mathfrak{B}
and
d
.

Again, our protocol operates with very few measurement settings.
In addition, the observable
O
’s information is not employed during the experimental stage.
With this constraint, the best previously known protocol for PCE has a sample complexity of
\mathcal{O}(d)
for general
O
, but relies on collective measurements on multiple copies of
\rho

Grier et al. 2024
.
In contrast, our protocol achieves this efficiency using only single-copy measurements.

Figure 3:
Estimated fidelities between
|\mathrm{{gs}}\rangle
and the virtually cooled state
\rho^{t}/\mathrm{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{t}}}\right)
as functions of the inverse temperature
\beta
and coupling strength
h
. Here
\rho
is the Gibbs state of the 14-qubit TFIM, and
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
denotes the ground state. The parameters are chosen as (a)
J=h=1.0
and (b)
J=1.0
,
\beta=1.0
. Solid and dashed curves represent the exact fidelities. Each point represents the average fidelity estimated by CBNE with
N_{U}=1
and
N_{M}=10^{6}
over 100 independent trials, and error bars indicate the standard deviation.

Next, as a proof-of-principle demonstration, we apply the CBNE protocol to QVC
Cotler et al. 2019
;
Du et al. 2026
. This technique aims to characterize properties of an effective low-temperature state using measurements on a noisy thermal ensemble, without requiring physical cooling of the system. For a Gibbs state
\rho=\operatorname{e}^{-\beta H}\!\!/\!\Tr(\operatorname{e}^{-\beta H})
with inverse temperature
\beta
, the virtually cooled state
\rho^{(t)}:=\rho^{t}/p_{t}\propto\operatorname{e}^{-t\beta H}

corresponds to a system at a lower effective temperature. Since CBNE serves as an effective tool for estimating the properties of
\rho^{(t)}
, it can be used naturally for QVC. We numerically demonstrate this using the TFIM Hamiltonian, focusing on measuring the fidelity between the virtually cooled state and the ground state. Remarkably, as illustrated in Fig.
3
, CBNE yields accurate estimates with only a single measurement setting, significantly simplifying experimental implementation.

Estimation of PT moments
—
Beyond estimating nonlinear properties
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, our CBNE framework can be extended to measure PT moments of quantum states.
Consider a bipartition of the original
n
-qubit state
\rho
into two subsystems
A
and
B
, and assume without loss of generality that
their dimensions
d_{A}\geq d_{B}
.
The
t
-th PT moment is then defined as
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}:=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\rho_{AB}^{\top_{\!B}})^{t}}}\right]
, where
(\cdot)^{\top_{\!B}}
denotes partial transpose with respect to
B

Gray et al. 2018
;
Elben et al. 2020a
;
Zhou et al. 2020
.

Here we outline the key features of our
PT moment estimation
(PTME) protocol, with full details provided in the End Matter.
Unlike CBNE, which uses global unitaries acting on the entire system,
PTME applies random unitaries only to subsystem
A
, making it more experimentally accessible.
It then measures one part of
A
in the computational basis and performs Bell measurements on qubit pairs between the remaining part of
A
and subsystem
B
, as illustrated in Fig.
4
(a).
As before,
N_{U}
and
N_{M}
denote the number of distinct random unitaries used and the number of measurements per unitary, respectively.

Figure 4:
(a) Illustration of the PTME protocol. A random unitary
U
is applied to subsystem
A
, followed by computational-basis measurements on part of
A
and Bell measurements on paired qubits between
B
and the remaining part of
A
.
(b) Entanglement-detection probability for

\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+pI/d
,
where
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
is the ground state of a 20-qubit TFIM with
J=h=1
.
We take subsystem
A
as the first 18 qubits and
B
as the remaining 2 qubits.
Each point is the fraction of 100 independent PTME trials in which the estimated witness detects entanglement, with
N_{U}=1
and
N_{M}=10^{8}
.
Red diamonds indicate detection by at least one of
D_{3},D_{4},D_{5}
,
and the vertical dashed line marks the theoretical threshold at which at least one of
D_{3},D_{4},D_{5}
becomes positive.

Theorem 3
.

Suppose
t\geq 2
is a constant integer.
For any
0<\epsilon<1
, the PTME protocol can return
\epsilon
-additive error estimates for all

p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}},\dots,p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}\!
with high probability, provided that

d_{\!A}\epsilon\geq c_{1}
and

\displaystyle N_{U}\!=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,c_{2}\,\Xi_{2}}}\right\},\ \ N_{M}\!\geq c_{3}\,d_{\!A}d_{B}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Xi_{2}^{1/t},1}}\right\}\!

(10)

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
independent of
d_{A},d_{B},\epsilon
. Here,
\Xi_{2}
is a shorthand for
\Xi_{2}(d_{A},d_{B},\epsilon):=d_{B}/(d_{A}\epsilon^{2})
.

Notably, when subsystem
A
is sufficiently larger than
B
, the PTME protocol again requires only
N_{U}=1
measurement setting, greatly simplifying experimental implementation.
If instead
d_{A}
is small, similar to CBNE, single-setting estimation of PT moments can still be achieved by first introducing at most
2\log_{2}(\epsilon^{-1})+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)
ancillary qubits (initialized in a pure state
\rho_{a}
) to subsystem
A
, and then applying PTME to the extended state
\tilde{\rho}=\rho_{AB}\otimes\rho_{a}
.
This modification preserves the target value
\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\tilde{\rho}^{\top_{\!B}})^{t}}}\right]=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\rho_{AB}^{\top_{\!B}})^{t}}}\right]
and does not increase the sample cost.
Moreover, PTME achieves the best-known sample complexity for orders
t\geq 4
.
A detailed comparison with existing approaches is provided in SM Sec.
S7
.

We demonstrate the utility of PTME for entanglement detection using the
D_{m}
witness hierarchy
Neven et al. 2021
; see SM Sec.
S9
for details.
Each
D_{m}
is constructed from PT moments up to order
m
, and higher orders provide stronger witnesses but are typically harder to estimate experimentally.
PTME addresses this challenge by estimating several PT moments simultaneously from a single measurement setting.
As shown in Fig.
4
(b), combining the witnesses
D_{3},D_{4},D_{5}
leverages the statistical robustness of lower orders and the detection power of higher orders, thereby improving the overall detection probability and extending the detectable range of entangled states.

Conclusion
—
We developed the CBNE framework for the efficient and practical estimation of nonlinear properties of quantum states with a single measurement setting.
Beyond its versatility for a wide range of quantum information processing tasks, CBNE suggests that switching among multiple measurement bases is not fundamentally necessary for accessing a broad class of nonlinear state properties.
This observation opens intriguing directions for quantum learning and foundational studies.

Several directions merit further investigation.
First, extending our framework to more general nonlinear functions like
\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes t})
and
\Tr(O_{1}\rho^{t_{1}}\cdots O_{m}\rho^{t_{m}})
can broaden its applicability to quantum information and many-body physics
Vitale et al. 2024
;
Rath et al. 2021
;
Zhou and Liu 2024
; see SM Sec.
S8
for a preliminary exploration.
Second, it would be valuable to explore hybrid schemes that combine CBNE with limited collective operations
King et al. 2025
;
Chen et al. 2024
, potentially further improving sample efficiency.
Finally, investigating fundamental lower bounds on the sample cost of nonlinear estimation
Anshu et al. 2022
;
Chen et al. 2022
;
Gong et al. 2024
would help assess the optimality of our framework.

CBNE with an approximate unitary design ensemble
—
The CBNE protocol introduced in the main text employs random unitaries drawn from an exact
2(t+1)
-design ensemble
\mathcal{E}
, which generally requires circuit depths that scale polynomially with the number of qubits.
This raises the natural question of whether
approximate unitary designs
can be used instead, as such ensembles are much more experimentally feasible: Recent works
Schuster et al. 2025
;
LaRacuente and Leditzky 2026
have shown that for constant
k
, an
\mu
-approximate unitary
k
-design on
n
qubits can be implemented with circuit depth
\mathcal{O}(\log(n/\mu))
without using ancillary qubits. The depth can be further reduced to
\mathcal{O}(\log\log(n/\mu))
when ancillary qubits are available
Cui et al. 2025
. A more detailed introduction
to approximate unitary designs is given in SM Sec.
S1
.

Here, we affirmatively answer this question. First, for the estimation of quantum state moments,
the CBNE protocol using approximate designs maintains the performance stated in Theorem
1

(see SM Sec.
S2
for proof):

Proposition 1
.

Suppose
t>1
is a constant integer. For any
0<\epsilon<1
,
the CBNE protocol can return
\epsilon
-additive error estimates for all

p_{2},p_{3},\dots,p_{t}
with high probability, provided that:
(i)
the random unitaries are sampled from a
(c_{3}\epsilon^{2})
-approximate
2t
-design ensemble, and

\displaystyle(ii)\,N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\dfrac{c_{1}}{d\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\},\ (iii)\,N_{M}\geq c_{2}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},d}}\right\}.

(12)

Here
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
are constants independent of
d
and
\epsilon
.

Second, for estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
with approximate unitary design, we introduce a slight modification to CBNE protocol’s postprocessing.
Instead of
\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})
in Eq. (
4
), we now calculate
\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O)
and use
\hat{\xi}_{k}^{O}=\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U_{\!s}}(O)/N_{U}
as the estimate for
\xi_{k}^{O}
[defined in Eq. (
5
) with
O_{0}\mapsto O
]. This variant is technically convenient for controlling the bias induced by approximate designs.
Since
\xi^{O}_{k}
can be expanded as a polynomial in
p_{j}
and
\Tr(O\rho^{i})
for
1\leq i,j\leq k
, the quantities
\Tr(O\rho^{k})
can again be recovered sequentially from the estimated
p_{k}
and
\xi^{O}_{k}
.

As proved in SM Sec.
S3
, the performance of this variant CBNE protocol is guaranteed by the following theorem. Note that we assume without loss of generality that the observable
O
is positive semidefinite (
O\geq 0
); if not, we decompose
O
as
O=O_{+}-O_{-}
with
O_{\pm}\geq 0
and estimate
\Tr(O_{+}\rho^{t})
and
\Tr(O_{-}\rho^{t})
separately.

Proposition 2
.

Suppose
t\geq 1
is a constant integer and
O
is an observable with
O\geq 0
and
\Tr(O)\geq 1
.
For any
0<\epsilon<1
, the variant CBNE protocol can return
\epsilon
-error estimates for all

\big\{p_{k},\Tr(O\rho^{k})\big\}_{k=1}^{t}
with high probability, provided that:

(i)
the random unitaries are sampled from a
[c_{3}\epsilon^{2}\!/\!\Tr(O)^{2}]
-approximate
2(t+1)
-design ensemble, and

\displaystyle{(ii)}\ N_{U}

\displaystyle=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\dfrac{c_{1}\Tr(O)^{2}}{d\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\},

(13)

\displaystyle{(iii)}\ N_{M}

\displaystyle\geq c_{2}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}\Tr(O)^{2/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},d}}\right\}.

(14)

Here
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
are constants independent of
O,d,\epsilon
.

Compared to Theorem
1
, the numbers
N_{U}
and
N_{M}
differ only in the replacement of
\mathfrak{B}=\max\{\Tr(O_{0}^{2}),1\}
with
\Tr(O)^{2}
. Therefore, for low-rank observables
O
,
nonlinear estimation with approximate designs achieves performance close to exact designs while requiring much shallower circuits.
This relaxation significantly reduces the implementation overhead of CBNE and strengthens its near-term applicability.

PTME protocol
—
Here we provide more details on our PTME protocol.
The protocol estimates PT moments through symmetrized quantities that play the same role as
\zeta_{k}
in the CBNE protocol.
For integer
k\geq 2
, define

\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}:=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\rho_{AB}^{\otimes k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(R_{\pi})_{A}\otimes(R_{\pi}^{\top})_{B}}}\right]}}\right\},

(15)

where
(R_{\pi})_{A}
denotes the permutation operator acting on subsystem
A
of
k
copies, and similarly for
(R_{\pi}^{\top})_{B}
.
As in ordinary moment estimation,
\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
depends only on the cycle structure of
\pi
, and hence is a polynomial in the PT moments
\{p_{j}^{\mathrm{PT}}\}_{j\leq k}
:

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}}}{2},\quad\zeta_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}}}{3}+\frac{p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}}{2},\quad\dots

(16)

Hence, estimating
\{\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}\}_{k=2}^{t}
allows one to sequentially recover
p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}},\dots,p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
via Eq. (
16
).

Input:

N_{U}N_{M}
prepared state
\rho
and integer
t\geq 2
.

1:

for

s=1,2,\dots,N_{U}
,
do

2:

Randomly choose a unitary
U_{s}
on subsystem
A
from a
2t
-design ensemble.

3:

for

j=1,2,\dots,N_{M}
,
do

4:

Apply
U_{s}
to subsystem
A
of
\rho
, obtaining the rotated state
\rho_{U_{s}}=(U_{s}\otimes I_{B})\rho(U_{s}\otimes I_{B})^{{\dagger}}
.

5:

Measure the subsystem
A_{1}
of
\rho_{U_{s}}
in the computational basis, obtaining outcome
\hat{b}_{j}\in\{0,\dots,d_{A_{1}}-1\}
.

6:

Measure the subsystems
A_{2}
and
B
with the POVM
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{+},G_{-}\}
,
obtaining outcome
\hat{r}_{j}\in\{+1,-1\}
.
Here the POVM elements
G_{\pm}=(I\pm\mathbb{S})/2
, with
I
and
\mathbb{S}
being the identity and swap operators on
A_{2}\cup B
, respectively.

7:

end

for

8:

Compute the following estimator for all
k=2,3,\dots,t
using the measurement data

\mathbf{b}_{U_{s}}=\big\{\hat{b}_{1},\hat{b}_{2},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\big\}
and
\mathbf{r}_{U_{s}}=\{\hat{r}_{1},\dots,\hat{r}_{N_{M}}\}
:

\displaystyle\ \hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U_{\!s}}:=\frac{d_{\!A}^{\,k}}{k!\,d_{\!A_{1}}\binom{N_{M}}{k}}\sum_{i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}}\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{r}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{r}_{i_{k}}}}\right)\textbf{1}\big\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\big\}.\!

(17)

9:

end

for

10:

For
k=2,3,\dots,t
, compute

\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}:=\frac{1}{N_{U}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U_{\!s}}
as our estimate for
\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
.

11:

Substitute
\hat{\zeta}_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}},\dots,\hat{\zeta}_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
into Eq. (
16
), then solve the equations to obtain estimates
\hat{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
for
p_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
(
k=2,\dots,t
).

Algorithm 2

PTME protocol for estimating
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right\}_{k=2}^{t}

Output:

\hat{p}_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}},\dots,\hat{p}_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
.

By leveraging tools from representation theory,
we construct a signed collision estimator
\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}
for
\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
, as defined in Eq. (
17
).
This estimator processes measurement data obtained under a fixed unitary
U
, and can be computed efficiently classically (see SM Sec.
S6
).
As proved in SM Sec.
S5
,
\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}}}\right]=\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}+\mathcal{O}(d_{A}^{-1}),

(18)

so its bias is of order
\mathcal{O}(d_{A}^{-1})
, which is exponentially small in the number of qubits of subsystem
A
.
This guarantees accurate estimation of
\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
and hence the PT moments.

The complete PTME protocol is summarized in Algorithm
2
; see Fig.
4
(a) in the main text for an illustration.
Recall that the subsystem
A
is not smaller than
B
.
For protocol implementation, we further partition
A
into two disjoint subsystems
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, such that
d_{A}=d_{A_{1}}d_{A_{2}}
and
d_{A_{2}}=d_{B}
.
This dimensional matching ensures the POVM
\mathcal{G}=\{G_{+},G_{-}\}
used in Step 6 of Algorithm
2
is properly defined.

Note that the POVM elements
G_{+}
and
G_{-}
are projectors onto the
+1
and
-1
eigenspaces of the swap operator
\mathbb{S}
, respectively.
Since
\mathbb{S}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n_{B}}\mathbb{S}_{i}
admits a qubit-wise tensor-product form,
the POVM
\mathcal{G}
can be realized through
n_{B}
local 2-qubit projective measurements: For each pair of qubits across
A_{2}
and
B
, one performs the Bell measurement

\{I-|\Psi_{-}\rangle\langle\Psi_{-}|,|\Psi_{-}\rangle\langle\Psi_{-}|\}
, where

\displaystyle|\Psi_{-}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|01\rangle-|10\rangle),\ \ |\Psi_{-}\rangle\langle\Psi_{-}|=\frac{1}{2}(I_{i}-\mathbb{S}_{i}).

(19)

The overall measurement result of
\mathcal{G}
is determined by the parity of the outcome
|\Psi_{-}\rangle\langle\Psi_{-}|
counts: An even (odd) number of such outcomes corresponds to
+1
(
-1
).

Quantum Nonlinear Properties from a Single Measurement Setting:

Supplementary Material

August 10, 2026

S1
Preliminaries

For our proofs in the following context, in this section, we review some key concepts and notation about permutations, Haar measure, and (approximate) unitary designs.
A more detailed review can be found in Ref.
Mele 2024
.

Throughout the paper, we consider an
n
-qubit Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with dimension
d=2^{n}
. Denote by
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
the set of all density operators on
\mathcal{H}
, and by
\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
the set of linear operators on
\mathcal{H}
. Let
\openone
be the identity operator on
\mathcal{H}
, and
\{|b\rangle\}_{b=0}^{d-1}
be the computational basis of
\mathcal{H}
. For any
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
, we write
\|A\|
for its operator norm,
\|A\|_{1}
for its Schatten 1-norm, and
\|A\|_{2}
for its Schatten 2-norm (Frobenius norm).

Let
\mathrm{S}_{k}
denote the permutation group of order
k
. For any
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}
, we define
\#(\pi)
as the number of disjoint cycles in
\pi
,
and define the associated permutation operator
R_{\pi}
acting on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes k}
as the unitary operator satisfying

\displaystyle R_{\pi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\varphi_{1}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|\varphi_{k}\rangle}}\right)=|\varphi_{\pi^{-1}(1)}\rangle\otimes\cdots\otimes|\varphi_{\pi^{-1}(k)}\rangle\qquad\forall\,|\varphi_{1}\rangle,\ldots,|\varphi_{k}\rangle\in\mathcal{H}.

(S1)

Here, the action of
R_{\pi}
rearranges the tensor factors according to the inverse permutation
\pi^{-1}
. For instance, if
\pi=(1,2)\in\mathrm{S}_{2}
(the transposition swapping elements 1 and 2), then
R_{\pi}
is the swap operator, which we denote specifically by
\mathbb{S}
.
To describe permutations acting independently on sub-blocks, we write
(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\in\mathrm{S}_{2k}
for the permutation where
\tau_{1}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}
acts on the first
k
elements and
\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}
acts on the remaining
k
elements.

A Haar random unitary on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of dimension
d
is a unitary operator
U
drawn uniformly from the
Haar measure

\mu_{\rm H}
on the unitary group
{\rm U}(d)
. This measure is the unique unitarily invariant probability distribution satisfying

\displaystyle\underset{U\sim\mu_{\rm H}}{{\mathbb{E}}}[f(U)]:=\int_{{\rm U}(d)}f(U){\rm d}\mu_{\rm H}(U)=\int_{{\rm U}(d)}f(VU){\rm d}\mu_{\rm H}(U)=\int_{{\rm U}(d)}f(UV){\rm d}\mu_{\rm H}(U)

(S2)

for any integrable function
f
and fixed
V\in{\rm U}(d)
. Here we use
{{\mathbb{E}}}_{U\sim\mu_{\rm H}}[f(U)]
to denote the expectation of
f(U)
with respect to the Haar measure.
For any
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
, the
k
-fold Haar-random twirling channel
is defined as

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm H}(A):=\underset{U\sim\mu_{\rm H}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[U^{\otimes k}AU^{\dagger\otimes k}}}\right].

(S3)

When
A=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi}}\right|
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle\in\mathcal{H}
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm H}(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\psi}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\psi}}\right|)=\frac{1}{\kappa_{k}}\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(k)}=\frac{1}{k!\,\kappa_{k}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}R_{\pi},\qquad\kappa_{k}=\binom{k+d-1}{k},

(S4)

where
\Pi_{\operatorname{sym}}^{(k)}
is the projector onto the symmetric subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes k}
,
and
\kappa_{k}
is the dimension of this symmetric subspace.
For general
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathcal{H})
, the
k
-fold twirling result reads

\displaystyle\Phi_{\rm H}(A)=\sum_{\tau,\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,\tau}^{(k,d)}\Tr(AR_{\pi}^{\top})R_{\tau},

(S5)

where
(\cdot)^{\top}
denotes the transpose operation (with respect to the computational basis), and

\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,\tau}^{(k,d)}\in\mathbb{R}
is known as the
Weingarten function
.
When the space dimension
d
is large, the Weingarten function scales as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,\tau}^{(k,d)}=d^{-k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\delta_{\pi,\tau}+\mathcal{O}(d^{-1})}}\right).

(S6)

This equation holds because the permutation operators are asymptotically orthogonal in the limit of
d\rightarrow\infty

Köstenberger 2021
;
Aharonov et al. 2022
.

Generating Haar-random unitaries on quantum computers is inefficient, since most unitaries require an exponential number of elementary gates with respect to the system size
n
. This motivates the definition of (approximate) unitary
k
-design, which are unitary ensembles
\mathcal{E}
that (approximately) replicates the statistical properties of the Haar measure up to the
k
-th moment.
Formally,
\mathcal{E}
is called a
unitary
k
-design
if
\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}=\Phi_{\rm H}
, where

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}(A):=\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[U^{\otimes k}AU^{\dagger\otimes k}}}\right]

(S7)

is the
k
-fold twirling channel with respect to
\mathcal{E}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{E}
is called an
\epsilon
-approximate unitary
k
-design
if

\displaystyle(1-\epsilon)\Phi_{\rm H}\preccurlyeq\Phi_{\mathcal{E}}\preccurlyeq(1+\epsilon)\Phi_{\rm H},

(S8)

where
\Phi\preccurlyeq\Phi^{\prime}
denotes that
\Phi^{\prime}-\Phi
is a completely-positive map.
By definition, any (
\epsilon
-approximate) unitary
k
-design is automatically a (
\epsilon
-approximate) unitary
(k-1)
-design.
Recent works
Schuster et al. 2025
;
LaRacuente and Leditzky 2026
have proved that for constant
k
, an
\epsilon
-approximate unitary
k
-design on
n
qubits can be efficiently constructed in circuit depth
\mathcal{O}(\log(n/\epsilon))
, without using ancilla qubits. When ancilla qubits are permitted, the required depth can be further reduced to
\mathcal{O}(\log\log(n/\epsilon))

Cui et al. 2025
.

S2
Estimation of quantum state moments with the CBNE protocol

In this section, we provide further details on the CBNE protocol for quantum state moment estimation. While Algorithm
1
in the End Matter summarizes the full CBNE protocol, for the analysis in this section we isolate its moment-estimation part and present it as Algorithm
S1
, which should be viewed as a proof-oriented subroutine rather than a separate protocol.

Recall that the quantity
\zeta_{k}
[defined in Eq. (
2
) of the main text] can be expressed as a polynomial of
p_{j}
with
j\leq k
:

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{p_{2}}{2},\qquad\zeta_{3}=\frac{1}{6}+\frac{p_{2}}{3}+\frac{p_{3}}{2},\qquad\zeta_{4}=\frac{1}{24}+\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{8}+\frac{p_{2}}{4}+\frac{p_{3}}{3}+\frac{p_{4}}{4},\qquad\dots

(S9)

Consequently, in Step 10 of Algorithm
S1
, one can sequentially invert these relations with the estimated
\zeta_{k}
to obtain the desired moment values:

\displaystyle p_{2}=2\zeta_{2}-1,\qquad p_{3}=2\zeta_{3}-\frac{1}{3}-\frac{2p_{2}}{3},\qquad p_{4}=4\zeta_{4}-\frac{1}{6}-\frac{p_{2}^{2}}{2}-p_{2}-\frac{4p_{3}}{3},\qquad\dots

(S10)

The rest of this section is organized as follows:
In Sec.
A
, we derive the expectation and variance of our estimator
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
in Eq. (
S11
). In Sec.
B
, we prove the performance of CBNE for state moment estimation as stated in Theorem
1
of the main text. In Sec.
C
, we prove Proposition
1
in the End Matter, which demonstrates the performance of CBNE for state moment estimation when the unitary ensemble
\mathcal{E}
is an approximate unitary design.

Input:

N_{U}N_{M}
sequentially prepared quantum state
\rho
and integer
t\geq 2
.

1:

for

s=1,2,\dots,N_{U}
,
do

2:

Randomly choose a unitary
U_{s}
from an ensemble
\mathcal{E}
.

3:

for

j=1,2,\dots,N_{M}
,
do

4:

Evolve the state
\rho
using
U_{s}
to get
U_{s}\rho U_{s}^{{\dagger}}
.

5:

Measure the rotated state in the computational basis, obtaining outcome
\hat{b}_{j}\in\{0,1,\dots,d-1\}
.

6:

end

for

7:

Compute the following collision estimator for all
k=2,\dots,t
using the measurement data
\mathbf{b}_{U_{s}}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
:

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{k}^{U_{s}}:=\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d\binom{N_{M}}{k}}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}}\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\},

(S11)

8:

end

for

9:

For
k=2,\dots,t
, compute the average

\hat{\zeta}_{k}:=\frac{1}{N_{U}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{M}_{k}^{U_{s}}
as our estimate for
\zeta_{k}
.

10:

Substitute
\hat{\zeta}_{2},\dots,\hat{\zeta}_{t}
into Eq. (
S9
), then solve the equations to obtain estimates
\hat{p}_{k}
for
p_{k}
(
k=2,\dots,t
).

Algorithm S1

CBNE protocol for estimating
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p_{t}}}\right\}_{k=2}^{t}

Output:

\hat{p}_{2},\dots,\hat{p}_{t}
.

A
Expectation and variance of
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}

The statistical fluctuation of our estimator
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
(see Eq. (
S11
)) comes from:
(i) the randomness in the selection of
U
, and
(ii) the randomness in quantum measurement outcomes
\mathbf{b}_{U}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
according to Born’s rule.
Taking these two sources of randomness into account, the expectation and variance of
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
are
clarified by the following two lemmas.

Lemma S1
.

Suppose
U
is a random unitary sampled from a
k
-design ensemble
\mathcal{E}_{k}
.
Then
{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]=\zeta_{k}
.

Lemma S2
.

Suppose
k>1
is a constant integer independent of the system dimension
d
, and
U
is a random unitary sampled from a
2k
-design ensemble
\mathcal{E}_{2k}
.
Then

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right).

(S12)

Proof of Lemma
S1
.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{M}_{k}^{U}\Big|U}}\right)=\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}=b\,|U}}\right)}}\right]=\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\Pr}_{\rho}(b|U)^{k}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}}}\right]=\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}R_{\pi}}}\right)=\zeta_{k},

(S13)

where the equality
(a)
follows from Eq. (
S4
) and the relation
\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}=\Phi_{\rm H}
.
This confirms Lemma
S1
, implying that
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
is an unbiased estimator of
\zeta_{k}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
S2
.

The variance of
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
reads

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]^{2}={\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right)^{2}\Big|U}}\right]-\zeta_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}}}\right)^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}\\
1\leq j_{1}<j_{2}<\dots<j_{k}\leq N_{M}\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)-\zeta_{k}^{2}.

(S14)

where the second equality follows from Lemma
S1
.

Note that
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
are two
k
-tuples drawn from the index set
\{1,2,\dots,N_{M}\}
. Let
{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]
be the collision number between them, that is, the number of common elements shared by
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
and
(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})
.
For
l\in\{0,1,\dots,k-1\}
, if

{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]=k-l
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)={\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}=\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{U}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k+l}=\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes(k+l)}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes(k+l)}\big)}}\right]=\frac{d}{\kappa_{k+l}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes{(k+l)}}\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(k+l)}}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{1-k-l}}}\right),

(S15)

where the last equality follows because
\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes{(k+l)}}\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{(k+l)}}}\right]\leq 1
and that

\kappa_{k+l}=\binom{k+l+d-1}{k+l}=\Theta(d^{k+l})
when
k
and
l
are both constant independent of
d
.
If
{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]=0
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)={\mathbb{E}}_{U}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\langle b^{\prime}|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b^{\prime}\rangle^{k}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{U}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{2k}+{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\langle b^{\prime}|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b^{\prime}\rangle^{k}=d^{2-2k}k!^{2}\zeta_{k}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{1-2k}}}\right),

(S16)

where the last equality holds because

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{2k}=\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes 2k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes 2k}\big)}}\right]=\frac{d}{\kappa_{2k}}\mathrm{tr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2k}\Pi_{\mathrm{sym}}^{2k}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}(d^{1-2k})

(S17)

and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{U}\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\langle b^{\prime}|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b^{\prime}\rangle^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b^{\prime}}}\right|^{\otimes k}}}\right)\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2k}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}}\sum_{\tau,\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{2k}}\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,\tau}^{(2k,d)}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b^{\prime}}}\right|^{\otimes k}}}\right)R_{\tau}}}\right]\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2k}R_{\pi}^{\top}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{2k}}\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{(2k,d)}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2k}R_{\pi}}}\right)

\displaystyle=d(d-1)\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\mathrm{Wg}_{(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}),(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{(2k,d)}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2k}R_{(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}}}\right)+d(d-1)\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{2k}\\
\pi\neq(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\end{subarray}}\mathrm{Wg}_{\pi,(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})}^{(2k,d)}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2k}R_{\pi}}}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}d(d-1)\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[d^{-2k}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{-2k-1}}}\right)}}\right]\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}R_{\tau_{1}}}}\right)\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}R_{\tau_{2}}}}\right)+d(d-1)\sum_{\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{2k}\\
\pi\neq(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\end{subarray}}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{-2k-1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=d^{2-2k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\sum_{\tau\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes t}R_{\tau}}}\right)}}\right]^{2}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{1-2k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=d^{2-2k}k!^{2}\zeta_{k}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{1-2k}}}\right).

(S18)

Here,

(a)
follows from Eq. (
S5
);

(b)
holds because
\Tr\big[\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\!\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b^{\prime}}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)R_{\tau}\big]=1
if
\tau=(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
for some
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathrm{S}_{k}
, and 0 otherwise;

(c)
follows from Eq. (
S6
).

The variance in Eq. (
A
) is then upper bounded by

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]+\zeta_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}}}\right)^{2}\sum_{l=0}^{k}\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k},j_{1}<j_{2}<\dots<j_{k},\\
{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]=k-l\end{subarray}}{\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}}}\right)^{2}\,\sum_{l=0}^{k}\binom{N_{M}}{k+l}\binom{k+l}{k}\binom{k}{l}\,{\mathbb{E}}_{U}{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathbf{b}_{U}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)\Big|_{{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]=k-l}

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\,\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\binom{N_{M}}{k+l}\binom{k+l}{k}\binom{k}{l}\,\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{1-k-l}}}\right)+\binom{N_{M}}{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[d^{2-2k}k!^{2}\zeta_{k}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{1-2k}}}\right)}}\right]}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\binom{N_{M}}{k+l}\mathcal{O}(d^{k-1-l})+\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\binom{N_{M}}{2k}\binom{2k}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{k!\kappa_{k}}{d^{k}}}}\right)^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\zeta_{k}^{2}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{-1}}}\right)}}\right],

(S19)

where the third equality follows from Eqs. (
A
) and (
A
).
Note that

\displaystyle\frac{k!\kappa_{k}}{d^{k}}=\frac{k!}{d^{k}}\binom{k+d-1}{k}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{k-1}{d}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{k-2}{d}}}\right)\cdots\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{1}{d}}}\right)=1+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{-1}}}\right)

(S20)

and

\displaystyle\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\binom{N_{M}}{2k}\binom{2k}{k}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{N_{M}!}{k!(N_{M}-k)!}}}\right]^{-2}\frac{N_{M}!}{(2k)!(N_{M}-2k)!}\cdot\frac{(2k)!}{(k)!(k)!}=\frac{(N_{M}-k)!^{2}}{N_{M}!(N_{M}-2k)!}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{N_{M}-k}{N_{M}}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{N_{M}-k-1}{N_{M}-1}}}\right)\cdots\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{N_{M}-2k+1}{N_{M}-k+1}}}\right)\leq 1.

(S21)

By plugging these into Eq. (
A
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]+\zeta_{k}^{2}\leq\sum_{l=0}^{k-1}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d^{k-l-1}}{N_{M}^{k-l}}}}\right)+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[1+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{-1}}}\right)}}\right]^{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\zeta_{k}^{2}+\mathcal{O}(d^{-1})}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right)+\zeta_{k}^{2},

(S22)

which confirms Lemma
S2
.
∎

B
Proof of CBNE’s performance in state moment estimation

When applied to state moment estimation,
the performance of the CBNE protocol is summarized in the following theorem, which is a restatement of Theorem
1
from the main text in the special case of
O=\openone
.

Theorem 4
.

Suppose
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
t>1
is a constant integer independent of the system dimension
d
.
For any
0<\epsilon<1
, the CBNE protocol (in Algorithm
S1
) can return
\epsilon
-additive error estimates for all

p_{2},p_{3},\dots,p_{t}
with high probability, provided that:

(i)
the random unitaries are sampled from a
2t
-design ensemble,

\displaystyle(ii)\ N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\dfrac{c_{1}}{d\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\},\quad\text{and}\quad(iii)\ N_{M}\geq c_{2}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},d}}\right\}.

(S23)

Here
c_{1},c_{2}>0
are some constants independent of
d
and
\epsilon
.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

Let
0<\delta<1
be a constant.
According to the union bound and Lemma
S3
below, in order to estimate all

p_{2},\dots,p_{t}
within
\epsilon
additive error and failure probability at most
\delta
, it
suffices to ensure that the following condition holds for all
k=2,\dots,t
:

\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}-\zeta_{k}}}\right|>c\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\frac{\delta}{t},

(S24)

where
c>0
is some constant factor independent of
\epsilon
and
d
.

By Lemma
S1
,
\hat{\zeta}_{k}=\frac{1}{N_{U}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{M}_{k}^{U_{s}}
is an unbiased estimator for
\zeta_{k}
.
Then Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}-\zeta_{k}}}\right|>c\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\frac{1}{c^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\zeta}_{k}}}\right]=\frac{1}{c^{2}\epsilon^{2}N_{U}}\Var\!\Big[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}\Big]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right),

(S25)

where the last equality follows from Lemma
S2
.
Hence, to make Eq. (
S24
) hold, it is sufficient to take

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}d}\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{d^{k-1}}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}N_{\!M}^{k}}\leq 1,

(S26)

where we omit constant coefficients
c,t,\delta
.
It is straightforward to verify that these two conditions are satisfied by choosing
N_{U}
and
N_{M}
as in Theorem
4
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma S3
.

Suppose
t\geq 2
is a constant integer independent of the system dimension
d
. For any
0<\eta<1
, if the estimates
\{\hat{\zeta}_{k}\}_{k=2}^{t}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}-\zeta_{k}}}\right|\leq\eta\quad\forall k=2,\dots,t,

(S27)

then
\{\hat{p}_{k}\}_{k=2}^{t}
computed in Step 10 of Algorithm
S1
satisfy

|\hat{p}_{k}-p_{k}|=\mathcal{O}(\eta)
for all
k=2,\dots,t
.

Proof of Lemma
S3
.

We proceed by induction on
t
.

Base case (
t=2
):

From the relation
\zeta_{2}=1/2+p_{2}/2
, we see that estimating
\zeta_{2}
within
\eta
-additive error directly yields an
\mathcal{O}(\eta)
-additive error estimate for
p_{2}
. This establishes the lemma for
t=2
.

Inductive step (
t\geq 3
):

Now we assume that
t\geq 3
is a constant integer and the desired result holds for all integers smaller than
t
.
For any permutation
\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes t}R_{\pi}}}\right)=\begin{cases}p_{t}&\text{when }\#(\pi)=1,\\
\vskip-11.99998pt\\
\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}p_{i}^{\nu_{i}(\pi)}&\text{when }\#(\pi)\geq 2.\end{cases}

(S28)

Here
\#(\pi)
is the number of disjoint cycles in
\pi
, and the exponent
\nu_{i}(\pi)
denotes the number of disjoint cycles of length
i
in
\pi
, which satisfies
0\leq\nu_{i}(\pi)<t
(for
i=2,\dots,t-1
) when
\#(\pi)\geq 2
.
Combining Eq. (
S28
) with Eq. (
S11
), we derive:

\displaystyle t!\,\zeta_{t}=\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{t}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes t}R_{\pi}}}\right)=(t-1)!\,p_{t}+\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{t},\#(\pi)\geq 2}\,\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}p_{i}^{\nu_{i}(\pi)},

(S29)

where the first term uses the fact that the number of permutations in
\mathrm{S}_{t}
with
\#(\pi)=1
is
(t-1)!
.
Rearranging this relation yields:

\displaystyle p_{t}=t\,\zeta_{t}-\frac{1}{(t-1)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{t},\#(\pi)\geq 2}\,\prod_{i=2}^{t-1}p_{i}^{\nu_{i}(\pi)}.

(S30)

By the induction hypothesis, if Eq. (
S27
) holds, then the estimates
\{\hat{p}_{k}\}_{k=2}^{t-1}
obtained from
\{\hat{\zeta}_{k}\}_{k=2}^{t-1}
satisfy
|\hat{p}_{k}-p_{k}|=\mathcal{O}(\eta)
.
Since the RHS of Eq. (
S30
) is a summation of a constant number of terms,
substituting these
\{\hat{p}_{k}\}_{k=2}^{t-1}
and
\hat{\zeta}_{t}
into Eq. (
S30
) produces an estimate
\hat{p}_{t}
for
p_{t}
within error
\mathcal{O}(\eta)
. This completes the inductive step and the proof.
∎

C
Moment estimation with an approximate unitary design ensemble

This subsection aims to prove Proposition
1
in the End Matter.
To this end, we first analyze the expectation and variance of our estimator
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
(see Eq. (
S11
)) in the case where
U
is drawn from an approximate unitary design ensemble.

Lemma S4
.

Suppose
U
is a random unitary sampled from a
\mu
-approximate
k
-design ensemble
\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mu}
, then

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]-\zeta_{k}}}\right|\leq\mu\,\zeta_{k}.

(S31)

Proof of Lemma
S4
.

We have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]-\zeta_{k}}}\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{M}_{k}^{U}\Big|U}}\right)-\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{M}_{k}^{U}\Big|U}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\,\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}}}\right)-\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\,\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mu}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]}}\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\mu\,\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\frac{\mu}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}R_{\pi}}}\right)=\mu\,\zeta_{k},

(S32)

which confirms Lemma
S4
.
Here,
(a)
follows from Lemma
S1
;

(b)
follows from the definition of the
\mu
-approximate unitary
k
-design in Eq. (
S8
);
(c)
follows from Eq. (
S4
) and the relation
\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}=\Phi_{\rm H}
.
∎

Lemma S5
.

Suppose
k>1
is a constant integer and
U
is a random unitary sampled from a
\mu
-approximate
2k
-design ensemble
\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}
with
\mu\leq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{\Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right)+3\mu.

(S33)

Proof of Lemma
S5
.

Similar to Eq. (
A
),
the variance of
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
can be expanded as

\displaystyle\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{\Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]=\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right)^{2}\Big|U}}\right]-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}}}\right)^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}\\
1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leq N_{M}\end{subarray}}\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}}}\right)^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}\\
1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leq N_{M}\end{subarray}}(1+\mu)\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)-\big[(1-\mu)\zeta_{k}\big]^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}(1+\mu)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{\Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]+\zeta_{k}^{2}}}\right)-(1-\mu)^{2}\zeta_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle=(1+\mu)\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{\Var}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]+\mu(3-\mu)\zeta_{k}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right)+3\mu.

(S34)

Here,
(a)
holds because
\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu},\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}_{k}^{U}}}\right]\geq(1-\mu)\zeta_{k}\geq 0
(see Lemma
S4
) and

\displaystyle\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)\leq(1+\mu)\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big),

(S35)

which is proved below;
(b)
follows from Eq. (
A
), and
(c)
follows from Lemma
S2
.

To prove Eq. (
S35
), we consider two cases. First, if

{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]=k-l
with
l=0,\dots,k-1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)-\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)}}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k+l}-\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k+l}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes(k+l)}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes(k+l)}\big)}}\right]-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes(k+l)}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes(k+l)}\big)}}\right]}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\,\mu\,\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes(k+l)}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes(k+l)}\big)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mu\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big),

(S36)

which confirms Eq. (
S35
).
Here the second inequality follows from Eq. (
S8
).
Second, if
{\rm Co}[(i_{1},\dots,i_{k});(j_{1},\dots,j_{k})]=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)-\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big)}}\right|

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\langle b^{\prime}|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b^{\prime}\rangle^{k}-\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=0}^{d-1}\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\langle b^{\prime}|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b^{\prime}\rangle^{k}}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=0}^{d-1}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes 2k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}^{\mu}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\!\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b^{\prime}}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]-\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes 2k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\!\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b^{\prime}}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{b,b^{\prime}=0}^{d-1}\,\mu\,\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes 2k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{2k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\!\otimes\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b^{\prime}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b^{\prime}}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mu\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}_{2k}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\Big(\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right]\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{b}_{j_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{j_{k}}}}\right]\Big|U\Big),

(S37)

which confirms Eq. (
S35
) again.
This completes the proof of Lemma
S5
.
∎

We now prove Proposition
1
via Lemmas
S4
and
S5
.

Proposition S1

(Restatement of Proposition
1
)
.

Suppose
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H})
and
t>1
is a constant integer independent of the system dimension
d
.
For any
0<\epsilon<1
, the CBNE protocol (in Algorithm
S1
) can return
\epsilon
-additive error estimates for all

p_{2},p_{3},\dots,p_{t}
with high probability, provided that:
(i)
the random unitaries are sampled from a
(c_{3}\epsilon^{2})
-approximate
2t
-design ensemble,

\displaystyle(ii)\ N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\dfrac{c_{1}}{d\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\},\quad\text{and}\quad(iii)\ N_{M}\geq c_{2}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},d}}\right\},

(S38)

where
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
are constants independent of
d
and
\epsilon
.

Proof of Proposition
S1
.

Let
0<\delta<1
be a constant.
According to the union bound and Lemma
S3
, in order to estimate all

p_{2},\dots,p_{t}
within
\epsilon
additive error and failure probability at most
\delta
, it
suffices to ensure that the following condition holds for all
k=2,3,\dots,t
:

\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}-\zeta_{k}}}\right|>c\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\frac{\delta}{t},

(S39)

where
0<c<1
is some constant factor independent of
\epsilon
and
d
.
Note that Eq. (
S39
) can be achieved if

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\mathbb{E}}[\hat{\zeta}_{k}]-\zeta_{k}}}\right|<\frac{c\epsilon}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}[\hat{\zeta}_{k}]}}\right|>\frac{c\epsilon}{2}}}\right\}\leq\frac{\delta}{t}\quad\forall k=2,3,\dots,t.

(S40)

Assume that the random unitaries used in the CBNE protocol are sampled from a

(c^{2}\delta\epsilon^{2}/20t)
-approximate
2t
-design ensemble.
Then Lemma
S4
implies that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\mathbb{E}}[\hat{\zeta}_{k}]-\zeta_{k}}}\right|=\Big|{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{M}^{U}_{k}}}\right]-\zeta_{k}\Big|\leq\frac{c^{2}\delta\epsilon^{2}}{20t}\leq\frac{c\epsilon}{2}.

(S41)

In addition, Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}-{\mathbb{E}}[\hat{\zeta}_{k}]}}\right|>\frac{c\epsilon}{2}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{c^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\Var[\hat{\zeta}_{k}]=\frac{4}{c^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\cdot\frac{1}{N_{U}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right)+\frac{3c^{2}\delta\epsilon^{2}}{20t}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d},\frac{d^{k-1}}{N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right)+\frac{3\delta}{5t},

(S42)

where the second inequality follows from Lemma
S5
.
Therefore, to make sure that Eq. (
S40
) holds, it is sufficient to take

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}dN_{U}}\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{d^{k-1}}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}N_{\!M}^{k}}\leq 1,

(S43)

where we omit constant coefficients, including
\delta
and
t
.
It is straightforward to verify that these two conditions are satisfied by choosing
N_{U}
and
N_{M}
as in Eq. (
12
).
This completes the proof of Proposition
S1
.
∎

S3
Estimation of
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
with the CBNE protocol

In this section, we provide further details on the CBNE protocol for estimating nonlinear quantities of the form
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
.
The complete protocol is summarized in Algorithm
1
in the End Matter.
We also present in Algorithm
S2
a closely related variant of the CBNE protocol, which will be used in the analysis with approximate unitary designs.

The following quantity [introduced in Eq. (
5
) of the main text] will be frequently used:

\xi_{k}^{O}:=\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k+1}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}\otimes O}}\right)R_{\pi}}}\right],

(S44)

which
can be expressed as a polynomial of
p_{i}
and
\Tr(O\rho^{j})
with
1\leq i,j\leq k
:

\displaystyle\xi_{1}^{O}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr(O\rho)+\Tr(O)}{2},

\displaystyle\xi_{2}^{O}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Tr(O\rho)+\Tr(O\rho^{2})}{3}+\frac{(1+p_{2})\Tr(O)}{6},

\displaystyle\xi_{3}^{O}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+p_{2})\Tr(O\rho)}{8}+\frac{\Tr(O\rho^{2})+\Tr(O\rho^{3})}{4}+\frac{(1+3p_{2}+2p_{3})\Tr(O)}{24},

\displaystyle\dots\dots

(S45)

The rest of this section is organized as follows:
In Sec.
A
, we introduce an auxiliary function and an lemma.
In Sec.
B
, we derive the expectation and variance of our estimator
\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O)
[see Eqs. (
4
) and (
S46
)].
In Sec.
C
, we prove the performance of CBNE as stated in Theorem
1
of the main text. In Sec.
D
, we prove Proposition
2
in the End Matter, which clarifies the performance of the variant CBNE protocol when the ensemble
\mathcal{E}
is an approximate unitary design.

Input:

N_{U}N_{M}
sequentially prepared quantum state
\rho
, observable
O
, and integer
t\geq 1
.

1:

for

s=1,2,\dots,N_{U}
,
do

2:

Randomly choose a unitary
U_{s}
from an ensemble
\mathcal{E}
and record it.

3:

for

j=1,2,\dots,N_{M}
,
do

4:

Evolve the state
\rho
using
U_{s}
to get
U_{s}\rho U_{s}^{{\dagger}}
.

5:

Measure the rotated state in the computational basis, obtaining outcome
\hat{b}_{j}\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
.

6:

end

for

7:

Compute the following collision estimator for all
k=1,\dots,t
using the measurement data
\mathbf{b}_{U_{s}}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
:

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U_{\!s}}(O)=\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d\binom{N_{M}}{k}}\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{k}}\,\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right\}\,\widetilde{\Pr}_{O}(\hat{b}_{i_{1}}|U_{s}),

(S46)

where
\widetilde{\Pr}_{O}(b|U)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|UOU^{{\dagger}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle
.

8:

end

for

9:

For
k=1,2,\dots,t
, compute
\hat{\xi}_{k}^{O}=\frac{1}{N_{U}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U_{\!s}}(O)
as our estimate for
\xi_{k}^{O}
.

10:

Run the post-processing of Algorithm
S1
to obtain estimates
\hat{p}_{k}
for
p_{k}
(
k=2,\dots,t
).

11:

Substitute the estimated values of
\xi_{1}^{O},\dots,\xi_{t}^{O}
and
p_{2},\dots,p_{t}
into Eq. (
S3
), then solve the equations to obtain estimates
\hat{o}_{k}
for
\Tr(O\rho^{k})
(
k=1,\dots,t
).

Algorithm S2

Variant CBNE protocol for estimating
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{p_{k},\Tr(O\rho^{k})}}\right\}_{k=1}^{t}

Output:

\hat{p}_{2},\dots,\hat{p}_{t}
and

\hat{o}_{1},\dots,\hat{o}_{t}
.

A
An auxiliary function

For later use, we define the following function for an Hermitian observable
O
:
