b
), we have

\displaystyle f_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbf{b}_{U},\mathbf{r}_{U},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k},j_{1},\dots,j_{k}}}\right\}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{b=0}^{d_{A_{1}}-1}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d_{\!A}^{\,-2k}}}\right)+\sum_{b\neq b^{\prime}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{k!^{2}}{d_{\!A}^{\,2k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right)^{2}+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d_{\!A}^{\,-2k-1}}}\right)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d_{A_{1}}d_{\!A}^{\,-2k}}}\right)+d_{\!A_{1}}^{\,2}d_{\!A}^{\,-2k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k!\,\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right)^{2},

(S135)

which confirms Eq. (
S121
) in Lemma
S19
.

C
Proof of Theorem
3
in the main text

Theorem 7

(Restatement of Theorem
3
)
.

Suppose
\rho\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B})
and

t\geq 2
is a constant integer independent of the system dimensions
d_{A}
and
d_{B}
.
For any
0<\epsilon<1
, the PTME protocol (given in Algorithm
2
of the End Matter) can return
\epsilon
-additive error estimates for all

p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}},\dots,p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}\!
with high probability, provided that:
(i)
the random unitaries are sampled from a
2t
-design ensemble, and

\displaystyle(ii)\ d_{\!A}\epsilon\geq c_{1},\qquad(iii)\ N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,c_{2}\,\Xi_{2}}}\right\},\qquad(iv)\ N_{M}\geq c_{3}\,d_{\!A}d_{B}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Xi_{2}^{1/t},1}}\right\}

(S136)

for some constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
independent of
d_{A},d_{B},\epsilon
. Here,
\Xi_{2}
is a shorthand for
\Xi_{2}(d_{A},d_{B},\epsilon)=d_{B}/(d_{A}\epsilon^{2})
.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Let
0<\delta<1
be a constant independent of
d_{A},d_{B}
and
\epsilon
.
According to the union bound and Lemma
S20
below, in order to estimate all

p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}},p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}},\dots,p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
within
\epsilon
additive error and failure probability at most
\delta
, it
suffices to ensure that the following condition holds for all
k=2,\dots,t
:

\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}-\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right|>c\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\frac{\delta}{t},

(S137)

where
c>0
is some constant factor independent of
d_{A},d_{B},\epsilon
, and
\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}=\frac{1}{N_{U}}\sum_{s=1}^{N_{U}}\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U_{s}}
is
our estimator for
\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
(see Algorithm
2
).
Note that Eq. (
S137
) can be achieved if the following two relations hold:

\displaystyle(a)\ \,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right]-\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right|<\frac{c\epsilon}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad(b)\ \Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}-{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right]}}\right|>\frac{c\epsilon}{2}}}\right\}\leq\frac{\delta}{t}.

(S138)

By Lemma
S17
,
\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
is
an estimator for
\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
with bias
\mathcal{O}(d_{\!A}^{\,-1})
.
Thus, the relation
(a)
in Eq. (
S138
) holds provided that
d_{\!A}\epsilon\geq c_{1}
for some constants
c_{1}>0
.

In addition, Chebyshev’s inequality implies that

\displaystyle\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}-{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right]}}\right|>\frac{c\epsilon}{2}}}\right\}\leq\frac{4}{c^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right]=\frac{4}{c^{2}\epsilon^{2}N_{U}}\Var\!\Big[\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}\Big]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{1}{d_{A_{1}}},\frac{d^{k}}{d_{A_{1}}N_{\!M}^{k}}}}\right\}}}\right),

(S139)

where
d=d_{A}d_{B}
, and the last equality follows from Lemma
S18
.
Hence, to make the relation
(b)
in Eq. (
S138
) hold, it is sufficient to take

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}d_{A_{1}}}\leq 1\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{d^{k}}{\epsilon^{2}N_{U}d_{A_{1}}N_{\!M}^{k}}\leq 1\quad\forall k=2,\dots,t,

(S140)

where we omit constant coefficients, including
\delta
and
t
.
It is straightforward to verify that these two conditions are satisfied by choosing
N_{U}
and
N_{M}
as in Theorem
7
.
This completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma can be derived by a similar induction argument used in the proof of Lemma
S3
.

Lemma S20
.

Suppose
t\geq 2
is a constant integer. For any
0<\eta<1
, if the estimates
\big\{\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}\big\}_{k=2}^{t}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\hat{\zeta}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}-\zeta_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}}}\right|\leq c\eta\quad\forall k=2,\dots,t,

(S141)

then the quantities
\big\{\hat{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}\big\}_{k=2}^{t}
computed in Step 11 of Algorithm
2
satisfy

\big|\hat{p}_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}-p_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}\big|=\mathcal{O}(\eta)
for all
k=2,\dots,t
.

S6
Classical computational cost in postprocessing

In this section, we analyze the classical computational cost of our CBNE and PTME protocols.

A
Computational cost of CBNE for state moment estimation

Here we analyze the classical computational complexity of the CBNE protocol (in Algorithm
S1
) for estimating
p_{t}
, where
t\geq 2
is a constant integer.
The postprocessing stage involves computing the collision estimator

\displaystyle\hat{M}_{k}^{U}=\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\}.

(S142)

for
k=2,3,\dots,t
across
N_{U}
distinct random unitaries
U
,
where
\mathbf{b}_{U}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
denotes the list of measurement outcomes with
\hat{b}_{i}\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
.

To evaluate this quantity efficiently, it is convenient to group the
indices
1\leq i\leq N_{M}
according to the value of
\hat{b}_{i}
.
For
j\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
, define

\theta_{j}=\bigl|\{i\,|\,\hat{b}_{i}=j\}\bigr|

to be the number of occurrences of the value
j
among the
N_{M}
outcomes.
A
k
-tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
contributes to the sum in Eq. (
S142
) if and only if all its associated outcomes correspond to the same value
j
.
Therefore, the summation can be rewritten as

\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\}=\sum_{j\,|\,\theta_{j}>0}\binom{\theta_{j}}{k}.

(S143)

To compute this expression, one first scans the list

\{\hat{b}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{M}}
and records the frequencies
\theta_{j}
of the observed values, which requires
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
time.
Note that the number of nonzero
\theta_{j}
is at most
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
.
The sum
\sum_{j}\binom{\theta_{j}}{k}
can then be computed by iterating over these values.
Because
k
is a constant, each binomial coefficient
\binom{\theta_{j}}{k}
can be evaluated in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)
time, yielding a total cost of
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
.
Finally, since the prefactor
\frac{\kappa_{k}}{d}\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-1}
in Eq. (
S142
) can also be computed in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)
time, the overall time cost for evaluating
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
is
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
.

Recall that
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
should be computed for
k=2,\dots,t
across
N_{U}
distinct
U
.
Therefore, the overall classical computational complexity for estimating
p_{t}
via CBNE is

T_{1}=N_{U}\cdot\sum_{k=2}^{t}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{U}N_{M}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}}}\right),

(S144)

where the second equality holds because
t
is a constant, and the last equality follows from Eq. (
9
) in the main text.
Note that this computational complexity has the same order as the sample complexity
N_{U}N_{M}
, and is therefore the best scaling one can expect.
We remark again that the information of the random unitaries
U
is not used in the postprocessing.

B
Computational cost of CBNE for estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})

Next, we analyze the classical computational complexity of the CBNE protocol (in Algorithm
1
of the End Matter) for estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, where
t\geq 1
is a constant integer.
The postprocessing stage involves computing both
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
in Eq. (
S142
) and

\displaystyle\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})=\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d}\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle r_{i_{1}}}}\right|UO_{0}U^{{\dagger}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|r_{i_{1}}}}\right\rangle,

(S145)

for
k=2,3,\dots,t
across
N_{U}
distinct random unitaries
U
, where
O_{0}
denotes the traceless part of the observable
O
.

To compute
\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})
efficiently, as analyzed in the previous subsection, it is convenient to group the indices according to the value of
\hat{b}_{i}
.
Let
\theta_{j}=\bigl|\{i\,|\,\hat{b}_{i}=j\}\bigr|

denote the number of occurrences of outcome
j\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
among the list
\mathbf{b}_{U}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
.
Then the summation in Eq. (
S145
) can be rewritten as

\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\}\,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle r_{i_{1}}}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|r_{i_{1}}}}\right\rangle=\sum_{j|\theta_{j}>0}\binom{\theta_{j}}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle.

(S146)

The number of nonzero

\theta_{j}
is at most
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
, which can be obtained by scanning the list

\{\hat{b}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{M}}
with
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
time.

For each
j
with
\theta_{j}>0
, we additionally need to compute the quantity

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle
.
Assuming that the
d\times d
matrix representation of
U
is accessible. Let
e_{j}
denote the
d
-dimensional computational-basis vector corresponding to
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle
.
Then

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle=v_{j}^{\dagger}O_{0}v_{j},\qquad v_{j}=U^{\dagger}e_{j}.

(S147)

Since
v_{j}
is simply the
j
-th column of
U^{\dagger}
, obtaining this vector requires
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d}}\right)
time.
Then the matrix-vector product
O_{0}v_{j}
requires
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{2}}}\right)
operations for a general dense matrix
O_{0}
, and the final inner product
v_{j}^{\dagger}(O_{0}v_{j})
costs
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d}}\right)
time.
Therefore, evaluating
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle
requires
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{2}}}\right)
time in the worst case.

If the observable
O_{0}
has additional structure, the computational cost can be further reduced.
For example, if
O_{0}
is sparse and has
{\rm nnz}(O_{0})
nonzero entries, then the matrix-vector multiplication
v_{j}^{\dagger}O_{0}v_{j}
can be performed in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\rm nnz}(O_{0})}}\right)
time, which leads to an overall
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\rm nnz}(O_{0})+d}}\right)
cost for computing
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle
.
Alternatively, if
O_{0}
has rank
r
, one can write
O_{0}=AB^{\dagger}
with
A,B\in\mathbb{C}^{d\times r}
.
In this case, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle=v_{j}^{\dagger}O_{0}v_{j}=(A^{\dagger}v_{j})^{\dagger}(B^{\dagger}v_{j}),\qquad v_{j}=U^{\dagger}e_{j},

(S148)

which can be evaluated in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(rd}}\right)
time.
In summary, the overall complexity for evaluating
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle
is

\tilde{T}(O_{0})=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\!\big\{d+{\rm nnz}(O_{0}),d\cdot{\rm rank}(O_{0})\big\}}}\right)

(S149)

Because
k
is a constant, each binomial coefficient
\binom{\theta_{j}}{k}
in Eq. (
S146
) can be evaluated in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)
time.
Thus, computing the sum

\sum_{j:\theta_{j}>0}\binom{\theta_{j}}{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle j}}\right|UO_{0}U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|j}}\right\rangle

costs
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}\tilde{T}(O_{0})}}\right)
time in total.
This leads to an
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}\tilde{T}(O_{0})}}\right)
overall time cost for evaluating
\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})
in Eq. (
S145
).
Recall that we need to compute both
\hat{M}_{k}^{U}
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{k}^{U}(O_{0})
for all
k=2,\dots,t
and for
N_{U}
distinct
U
.
Therefore, the overall classical computational complexity for estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
via CBNE is

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=T_{1}+N_{U}\cdot\sum_{k=2}^{t}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}\tilde{T}(O_{0})}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{U}N_{M}\tilde{T}(O_{0})}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}\mathfrak{B}^{1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},\,\frac{\mathfrak{B}}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}\cdot\min\!\Big\{d+{\rm nnz}(O_{0}),d\cdot{\rm rank}(O_{0})\Big\}}}\right),\qquad\mathfrak{B}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Tr(O_{0}^{2}),1}}\right\},

(S150)

where the second equality holds because
t
is a constant, and the third equality follows from Eq. (
9
) in the main text.

C
Computational cost of PTME for PT moment estimation

Here we analyze the classical computational complexity of the PTME protocol (in Algorithm
2
in the End Matter) for estimating
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
, where
t\geq 2
is a constant integer.
The postprocessing stage involves computing the estimator

\displaystyle\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}:=\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-1}\frac{d_{\!A}^{\,k}}{k!\,d_{\!A_{1}}}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{r}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{r}_{i_{k}}}}\right)\times\textbf{1}\big\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\big\},

(S151)

for
k=2,3,\dots,t
across
N_{U}
distinct random unitaries
U
,
where

\mathbf{b}_{U}=\big\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\big\}
and
\mathbf{r}_{U}=\{\hat{r}_{1},\dots,\hat{r}_{N_{M}}\}
denote the measurement outcomes with
\hat{r}_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
and
\hat{b}_{i}\in\{0,\dots,d_{A_{1}}-1\}
.

To evaluate this quantity efficiently, it is again convenient to group the
indices
1\leq i\leq N_{M}
according to the value of
\hat{b}_{i}
.
For each
j\in\{0,\ldots,d_{A_{1}}-1\}
, define

\theta_{j}=\bigl|\{i\,|\,\hat{b}_{i}=j\}\bigr|

to be the number of occurrences of the outcome
j
among the
N_{M}
outcomes, and let
\mathcal{S}_{j}=\{i\,|\,\hat{b}_{i}=j\}

denote the corresponding index set.
A
k
-tuple
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})
contributes to the summation in
Eq. (
S151
) only when all its associated outcomes correspond to the same value

j
.
Therefore, the summation can be rewritten as

\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}(\hat{r}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{r}_{i_{k}})\times\mathbf{1}\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\cdots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}\}=\sum_{j\,|\,\theta_{j}>0}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\\
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{j}\end{subarray}}\hat{r}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{r}_{i_{k}}.

(S152)

To compute this expression, one first scans the lists

\{\hat{b}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{M}}
and
\{\hat{r}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{M}}
and records the
values
\theta_{j}
together with the corresponding values
\hat{r}_{i}
.
This step requires
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
time.
Note that among the list
\{\hat{b}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{M}}
there can be at most
N_{M}
distinct
values of
\hat{b}_{i}
, so the number of nonzero
\theta_{j}
is at most
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
.
For each
j
with
\theta_{j}>0
, we need to compute

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\\
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{j}\end{subarray}}\hat{r}_{i_{1}}\cdots\hat{r}_{i_{k}}.

(S153)

Because
\hat{r}_{i}=\pm 1
and
k
is a constant, this quantity can be
computed in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\theta_{j}}}\right)
time.
Summing over all
j
as in Eq. (
S152
) thus costs

\sum_{j}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\theta_{j}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
time.
Since the prefactor

\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-1}\frac{d_{A}^{\,k}}{k!\,d_{A_{1}}}

in Eq. (
S151
) can also be evaluated in
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)
time,
the overall time cost for computing
\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}
is
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)
.

Recall that
\hat{\Lambda}_{k}^{U}
needs to be computed for

k=2,3,\dots,t
across
N_{U}
distinct
U
.
Thus, the total classical computational complexity for estimating

p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
via PTME is

T_{3}=N_{U}\cdot\sum_{k=2}^{t}\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{M}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N_{U}N_{M}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d_{\!A}d_{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{B}}{d_{A}\epsilon^{2}}}}\right)^{1/t},\frac{d_{B}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}}}\right),

(S154)

where the last equality follows from Theorem
3
in the main text.
Note that this computational complexity has the same order as the sample complexity
N_{U}N_{M}
, and is therefore the best scaling one can expect.
We remark again that the information of the random unitaries
U
is not used in the postprocessing.

S7
Comparison with previous works

Numerous methods have been proposed in the literature for estimating nonlinear state properties such as
p_{t}
,
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, and
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
. These methods typically rely on collective operations performed over multiple copies of
\rho

Quek et al. 2024
;
Liu and Wang 2025
;
Shin et al. 2025
;
Shi et al. 2025
;
Subaşı et al. 2019
;
Zhou and Liu 2024
;
Liu et al. 2026
;
Chen et al. 2025
;
Shi et al. 2025
;
Ye et al. 2025
;
Ekert et al. 2002
;
Bruni 2004
;
Zhang et al. 2026
;
Gray et al. 2018
;
Carteret 2005
;
Cornfeld et al. 2019
;
Liu et al. 2022
, employing diverse techniques such as generalized swap tests
Zhou and Liu 2024
;
Ekert et al. 2002
;
Bruni 2004
;
Carteret 2005
;
Cornfeld et al. 2019
, block encoding
Liu and Wang 2025
;
Zhang et al. 2026
, and deliberately constructed circuits
Quek et al. 2024
;
Liu et al. 2026
, among others.
Beyond the sample-access model, several studies have further explored scenarios where a purification of the unknown state
\rho
is available
Liu et al. 2024
;
Zhang et al. 2026
;
Chen et al. 2025
.

However, the primary focus of this work is estimation via
single-copy
measurements. Thus, we refrain from further elaboration on these multi-copy approaches. Instead, in the remainder of this section, we survey existing works on nonlinear property estimation using single-copy operations, and compare them with our results.
The number of measurement settings required by these protocols are summarized in Table
1
of the End Matter.

A
Sample-optimal estimation of
p_{2}
,
\Tr(\sigma\rho)
, and
\Tr(O\rho^{2})

The estimation of the quantum state purity
p_{2}=\Tr(\rho^{2})
and the inner product
\Tr(\sigma\rho)
between two unknown states with single-copy measurements has been studied in Refs.
Elben et al. 2019
;
Brydges et al. 2019
;
Elben et al. 2020b
;
Anshu et al. 2022
.
Their protocols rely on RMs, and have the following resource requirements for achieving an additive error
\epsilon

Anshu et al. 2022
:

\displaystyle N_{U}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\dfrac{1}{d\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}}}\right),\qquad N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\epsilon},d}}\right\}}}\right).

(S155)

Here
d
is the system dimension,
N_{U}
is the number of global random unitaries used, and
N_{M}
is the repetition number per unitary.
This performance matches that of our CBNE protocol stated in Theorem
1
of the main text with
t=2
.
The resulting sample complexity
N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{d})
achieves the optimal scaling with respect to
d
among protocols based on single-copy operations
Anshu et al. 2022
;
Chen et al. 2022
;
Gong et al. 2024
.
Notably, the protocol in Ref.
Anshu et al. 2022
utilizes a collision estimator and can be viewed as a special case of our CBNE protocol with
t=2
and
O=\openone
.

By generalizing the purity estimation protocol of Refs.
Elben et al. 2019
;
Brydges et al. 2019
, a recent work
Du et al. 2026
proposed an efficient protocol for estimating

\Tr(O\rho^{2})
, termed the
observable-driven RM
(ORM) protocol.
When
O
is a dichotomic observable (i.e., a Hermitian operator with eigenvalues
\pm 1
), the resource costs of ORM coincide with those in Eq. (
S155
).
For a general observable
O
with
\|O\|\leq 1
, the number of measurement settings required by ORM becomes

\displaystyle N_{U}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\dfrac{1}{d\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}\log(\epsilon^{-1})}}\right),

(S156)

and single-setting estimation is no longer achievable.

ORM attains the optimal sample complexity with respect to
d
when
\|O\|_{1}=\Theta(d)

Du et al. 2026
.
However, its experimental procedure depends on the target observable
O
and therefore cannot be applied to simultaneously estimate many different observables, unlike CSE and CBNE.
To address this issue, Ref.
Du et al. 2026
further proposed a
braiding RM
(BRM) protocol, whose experimental stage is independent of the observable
O
.
BRM requires
N_{U}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/\epsilon^{2}}}\right)
random unitaries and
N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sqrt{d\Tr(O^{2})}}}\right)
measurements per unitary to estimate
\Tr(O\rho^{2})
.
Compared with this approach, our CBNE protocol achieves improved performance in both the setting number
N_{U}
and sample cost
N_{U}N_{M}
, and enables the estimation of more general higher-order functions
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
.

B
Estimation of
p_{t}
with the vEB protocol

In Ref.
van Enk and Beenakker 2012
, van Enk and Beenakker (vEB) proposed the first protocol for estimating state moments
p_{t}=\Tr(\rho^{t})
using single-copy measurements.
The central idea of their protocol is as follows.
Suppose one performs RMs on the state
\rho
using a fixed random unitary
U
sufficiently many times.
From the measurement outcomes, one can obtain an accurate estimate of the outcome probabilities

\Pr_{\rho}(b|U)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|U\rho U^{\dagger}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle
for
b\in\{0,\dots,d-1\}
, where
d
is the system dimension.
The protocol then averages these probabilities over many random unitaries to estimate the quantities

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{k}(b):=\mathbb{E}_{U}[{\Pr}_{\rho}(b|U)^{k}],\qquad k=2,3\dots,t.

(S157)

Using properties of Haar-random unitaries, one obtains

\displaystyle\kappa_{k}\,\mathcal{R}_{k}(b)=\kappa_{k}\,\underset{U\sim\mathcal{E}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}}}\right]=\kappa_{k}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\rho^{\otimes k}\Phi_{\mathcal{E}_{k}}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes k}\big)}}\right]=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k}}\Tr(\rho^{\otimes k}R_{\pi}):=\zeta_{k},

(S158)

As discussed earlier, each
\zeta_{k}
can be expressed as a polynomial of the moments
p_{j}
with
j\leq k
.
Thus, by estimating
\zeta_{k}
for
k=1,2,\dots,t
and inverting these polynomial relations, one can sequentially recover the desired moments
p_{j}

van Enk and Beenakker 2012
.

Reference
van Enk and Beenakker 2012
did not analyze the sample complexity of the vEB protocol.
In fact, a direct plug-in implementation of the vEB idea can be costly.
Indeed, since
\kappa_{t}=\binom{d+t-1}{t}=\mathcal{O}(d^{t})
, Eq. (
S158
) implies that estimating
\zeta_{t}
with constant additive error requires estimating
\mathcal{R}_{t}(b)
, and thus the probabilities
\Pr_{\rho}(b|U)
, to accuracy
\mu=\mathcal{O}(d^{-t})
.
Achieving such precision requires at least on the order of
1/\mu^{2}=\mathcal{O}(d^{2t})
measurement repetitions for each chosen random unitary, leading to a large measurement overhead.

Although our CBNE protocol also estimates state moments through the quantities
\zeta_{k}
, it is conceptually different from the vEB protocol.
Instead of first estimating individual outcome probabilities
\Pr_{\rho}(b|U)
and then computing their powers, our protocol directly estimates the power sums of these probabilities through collision statistics.
This avoids the need for extremely precise probability estimation and leads to a substantially lower sample complexity.
Furthermore, our protocol extends beyond moment estimation to more general nonlinear functions, including
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, principal component properties, and PT moments
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
, thereby enabling broader applications.

C
Estimation of
p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}
with the ZZL protocol

In Ref.
Zhou et al. 2020
, Zhou, Zeng, and Liu (ZZL) proposed an efficient protocol for estimating the third-order PT moment

p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}=\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\rho_{AB}^{\top_{\!B}})^{3}}}\right]
via RMs, with
\rho_{AB}
being a quantum state on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
.
The required number of random unitaries (measurement settings) and the total sample complexity are

\displaystyle N_{U}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{(d_{A}d_{B})^{2/3}}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right),

(S159)

respectively
Zhou et al. 2020
.
Compared with this protocol, our PTME protocol offers advantages in several aspects.
First, PTME requires a significantly smaller setting number when
d_{A}\gg d_{B}
(see Theorem
3
in the main text).
Second, the sample complexity for estimating
p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}
with PTME reads

\displaystyle N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d_{B}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{A}d_{B}^{2}}{\epsilon}}}\right)^{2/3}}}\right\}}}\right],

(S160)

which is smaller than that of the ZZL protocol in the regime
d_{B}\leq\max\{\sqrt{d_{A}},1/\epsilon^{2}\}
.
Third, PTME enables the estimation of higher-order PT moments, whereas the ZZL protocol is restricted to
p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}
.
Fourth, PTME only needs local random unitaries on
\mathcal{H}_{A}
during the experimental stage, while the ZZL protocol requires global random unitaries on
\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}

Zhou et al. 2020
, which can be experimentally challenging in some scenarios.

To address the last issue mentioned above, ZZL also proposed a variant protocol for estimating
p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}

Zhou et al. 2020
.
This protocol applies local random unitaries on both
\mathcal{H}_{A}
and
\mathcal{H}_{B}
, followed by Bell-basis measurements on each qubit pair shared between systems
A
and
B
.
However, this variant protocol only applies to quantum states with
d_{A}=d_{B}
, and its statistical performance remains unclear.

D
Nonlinear property estimation with CSE

Classical shadow estimation (CSE) is a recently developed framework for efficiently predicting properties of an unknown quantum state
\rho
using RMs
Huang et al. 2020
.
In each experimental round of CSE, one first applies a unitary
U
randomly drawn from a suitable ensemble
\mathcal{E}
to the
d
-dimensional quantum state
\rho
, and then measures the rotated state
U\rho U^{\dagger}
in the computational basis.
The measurement outcome
\hat{b}
together with the applied unitary
U
constitutes the raw data of the experiment.
From this data one can construct a classical estimator
\hat{\rho}
, referred to as a classical snapshot, which reproduces the original state
\rho
in expectation:
\mathbb{E}[\hat{\rho}]=\rho
.
By collecting
N
independent snapshots
\{\hat{\rho}_{1},\dots,\hat{\rho}_{N}\}
, one obtains a classical representation of the state that enables the estimation of properties of
\rho
via classical postprocessing.
For example, when
\mathcal{E}
is a unitary 3-design (e.g., the Clifford group), the snapshot
\hat{\rho}
has the form

\displaystyle\hat{\rho}=(d+1)U^{{\dagger}}|\hat{b}\rangle\langle\hat{b}|U-\openone,

(S161)

and the number of samples for estimating a linear property
\Tr(O\rho)
within error
\epsilon
reads
N=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Tr(O_{0}^{2})/\epsilon^{2}}}\right)

Huang et al. 2020
.

A key feature of CSE, also shared by CBNE, is that the experimental stage is independent of the target property one wants to estimate.
This independence enables simultaneous prediction of numerous distinct properties
Elben et al. 2023
;
Huang et al. 2020
.
Consequently, classical shadows provide an efficient approach for characterizing quantum systems and have found numerous applications in quantum learning and quantum many-body physics.

Beyond linear functions,
CSE can naturally be used to predict nonlinear functions of quantum states

Elben et al. 2020a
;
Rath et al. 2021
;
Huang et al. 2020
;
Elben et al. 2023
;
Seif et al. 2023
;
Vermersch et al. 2024a
.
For instance, the moment
p_{t}=\Tr(\rho^{t})
can be estimated using
t
distinct snapshots

\hat{\rho}_{i_{1}},\dots,\hat{\rho}_{i_{t}}
obtained in different experimental rounds.
Due to their independence,
\Tr(\hat{\rho}_{i_{1}}\hat{\rho}_{i_{2}}\cdots\hat{\rho}_{i_{t}})
provides an unbiased estimate of
p_{t}
.
To reduce the estimation error, one may use
N
independent snapshots and average over all possible index tuples
Pelecanos et al. 2026
;
Elben et al. 2020a
:

\displaystyle\widehat{p_{t}}=\frac{1}{N(N-1)\dots(N-t+1)}\sum_{\text{distinct}\ i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{t}\in\{1,\dots,N\}}\Tr(\hat{\rho}_{i_{1}}\hat{\rho}_{i_{2}}\cdots\hat{\rho}_{i_{t}}).

(S162)

Similarly, unbiased estimators for more general quantities such as
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
and the PT moments
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
can be constructed in the same spirit
Seif et al. 2023
;
Elben et al. 2020a
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\Tr(O\rho^{t})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N-1)\dots(N-t+1)}\sum_{\text{distinct}\ i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{t}\in\{1,\dots,N\}}\Tr(O\hat{\rho}_{i_{1}}\hat{\rho}_{i_{2}}\cdots\hat{\rho}_{i_{t}}),

(S163)

\displaystyle\widehat{p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N-1)\dots(N-t+1)}\sum_{\text{distinct}\ i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{t}\in\{1,\dots,N\}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\rho}_{i_{1}}}}\right)^{\top_{\!B}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\rho}_{i_{2}}}}\right)^{\top_{\!B}}\cdots\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{\rho}_{i_{t}}}}\right)^{\top_{\!B}}}}\right].

(S164)

However, for general
t\geq 2
, the statistical performance of these estimators is not yet fully understood.
A detailed analysis is currently available only for the estimator
\widehat{p_{t}}
in Eq. (
S162
), as recently studied in Ref.
Pelecanos et al. 2026
.
Below we briefly review their result and derive the corresponding sample complexity under the additive error criterion.

For constant integer
t
, Ref.
(
Pelecanos et al. 2026
, Theorem 5.2)
shows that
\widehat{p_{t}}
has variance

\displaystyle\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\widehat{p_{t}}}}\right]=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d}{N}}}\right)^{t-j}\Tr(\rho^{2j})}}\right].

(S165)

By Chebyshev’s inequality,

\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widehat{p_{t}}-p_{t}}}\right|>\epsilon}}\right\}\leq\Var\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\widehat{p_{t}}}}\right]/\epsilon^{2}
.
Hence, to achieve the target accuracy
\epsilon
with high probability, one needs to choose

\displaystyle N=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d}{\epsilon^{2/t}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}}}\right)

(S166)

for a general quantum state
\rho
.
Here
N
characterizes both the number of measurement settings and the total number of samples consumed.
To our knowledge, this represents the best known scaling for estimating higher-order moments of a general quantum state using single-copy measurements prior to our work.
In comparison, the sample complexity of our CBNE protocol for estimating
p_{t}
is (see Eq. (
9
) in the main text):

\displaystyle N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d^{1-1/t}}{\epsilon^{2/t}},\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}}}\right\}}}\right).

(S167)

Thus improves upon the scaling in Eq. (
S166
) by a factor of
d^{1/t}
, implying that CBNE has a strictly better sample efficiency than CSE on nonlinear state moment estimation.

Moreover, CBNE uses substantially fewer measurement settings than direct CSE-based nonlinear estimators.
For moment estimation, the number of settings required by CBNE is only

N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,c/(d\epsilon^{2})}}\right\}

for some constant
c
, which is much smaller than the setting number in Eq. (
S166
).
This reduction is practically important: on many quantum platforms, repeated measurements under a fixed setting are relatively inexpensive, whereas changing the measurement basis can be time-consuming.
A similar distinction appears for estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
and
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
, where the number of required measurement settings also scales with the total sample cost
N
, which is exponentially large with the system size.
By contrast, CBNE and PTME have a much smaller setting complexity; see Table
1
.

Another limitation of direct CSE-based nonlinear estimation is its postprocessing cost.
Evaluating the estimators in Eqs. (
S162
), (
S163
), and (
S164
) requires constructing
N
snapshots
\hat{\rho}
and combining them over distinct
t
-tuples of indices.
A straightforward implementation therefore involves
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N^{t}}}\right)
tuple products and the corresponding matrix multiplications, which can become prohibitive when
N
is large.
By contrast, CBNE and PTME process the data directly through collision statistics.
As shown in Sec.
S6
, the postprocessing cost for estimating
p_{t}
and
p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}
scales only linearly in
N
,
and the applied random unitaries
U
need not be used in postprocessing, unlike CSE.
For estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
, the CBNE postprocessing cost is
\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(N\,\tilde{T}(O_{0})}}\right)
, where
\tilde{T}(O_{0})
is defined in Eq. (
S149
).
Thus, for the nonlinear quantities considered here, the collision-based estimators of our protocols substantially reduce the postprocessing overhead compared with
direct CSE snapshot-product estimators.

E
Single-setting CSE via ancillary systems

Two recent works
Tran et al. 2023
;
McGinley and Fava 2023
propose a variant of the CSE approach that enables property estimation using a single measurement setting.
In each experimental round of their protocol, the
n
-qubit state
\rho
of interest is first entangled with a sufficiently large ancillary system. Next, instead of applying randomly sampled unitaries, a fixed global unitary
U
or a fixed Hamiltonian time evolution is applied to the joint system consisting of
\rho
and the ancillas, followed by a measurement in the computational basis.
From the resulting measurement outcomes, one can construct classical shadow snapshots
\hat{\rho}
that remain unbiased estimators of
\rho
.
These snapshots can then be used in classical postprocessing to estimate various functions of
\rho
.
For linear properties, Ref.
McGinley and Fava 2023
shows that this variant protocol achieves a sample efficiency comparable to the standard CSE protocol, while its performance for nonlinear properties remains unclear.

The underlying principle of this approach is that, when the ancillary system is sufficiently large, the overall experimental procedure (adding ancillas, unitary evolution, and computational-basis measurement) effectively implements a
tomographically complete
POVM on the original state
\rho
.
Consequently, the full information of
\rho
can in principle be reconstructed from the measurement data.

The notable feature of this variant CSE approach, namely, property estimation from a single measurement setting, is similar to our CBNE and PTME protocols.
However, this advantage comes at the cost of requiring a large ancillary system, whose qubit number scales as (see Ref.
(
McGinley and Fava 2023
, Theorem 1)
):

\displaystyle n_{a}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n+\log(\epsilon^{-1})}}\right).

(S168)

Such a requirement can be prohibitive for near-term quantum devices when the system size
n
is large.
In contrast, our CBNE and PTME protocols require far fewer ancillary qubits.
As shown in the main text, in common scenarios our protocols achieve a single measurement setting without introducing any ancillary qubits.
Even in the worst case, the number of ancillary qubits needed is bounded by

\displaystyle n_{a}\leq 2\log_{2}(\epsilon^{-1})+\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right),

(S169)

which is completely independent of the system size
n
of the original system.
This represents a substantial reduction in ancillary-system overhead compared with Refs.
Tran et al. 2023
;
McGinley and Fava 2023
, particularly for large-scale quantum systems.

S8
Estimation of other nonlinear functions with the CBNE framework

In this section, we discuss how our CBNE framework can be extended beyond
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
,
\Tr(\rho^{t})
, and
\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[(\rho_{AB}^{\top_{\!B}})^{t}}}\right]
.
The main purpose here is to illustrate that the central idea of CBNE, namely, extracting information from collision statistics under single-copy randomized measurements, applies to a broader class of nonlinear quantities as well.
We present two representative examples,
showing how our framework can be adapted to estimate the expectation values of two-body observables and general multivariate functions.

A
Estimation of two-body observables

We first consider the estimation of
\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})
, where
\rho
is an unknown quantum state on
\mathcal{H}
and
O
is a two-body observable on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 2}
.

Suppose that copies of
\rho
are prepared sequentially.
In each experimental round, we apply a Haar-random unitary
U
to
\rho
, followed by a computational-basis measurement.
Repeating this procedure
N_{M}
times with the same unitary
U
produces a list of outcomes

\mathbf{b}_{U}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\hat{b}_{2},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
.
From these data, we define the following estimator:

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}^{U}:=\frac{24\kappa_{4}}{d}\binom{N_{M}}{2}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}\leq N_{M}}\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\hat{b}_{i_{2}}}}\right\}\cdot\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U^{{\dagger}}|\hat{b}_{i_{1}}\rangle\langle\hat{b}_{i_{1}}|U}}\right)^{\otimes 2}O}}\right].

(S170)

This estimator is again constructed from collision events, in the same spirit as the basic CBNE protocol.
As proved below, the expectation value of
\hat{\Delta}^{U}
is given by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Delta}^{U}}}\right]

\displaystyle=2\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})+2\Tr(\mathbb{S}O\rho^{\otimes 2})+[1+\Tr(\rho^{2})][\Tr(O)+\Tr(\mathbb{S}O)]+2\Tr(O_{1}\rho)+2\Tr(O_{2}\rho)

\displaystyle\quad\ +2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{1}\rho]+2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{2}\rho]+2\Tr(O_{1}\rho^{2})+2\Tr(O_{2}\rho^{2})+2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{1}\rho^{2}]+2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{2}\rho^{2}]

(S171)

where
\mathbb{S}
denotes the swap operator on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 2}
;

O_{1}:=\Tr_{2}(O)
and
O_{2}:=\Tr_{1}(O)

denote the reduced operators on the first and second subsystem, respectively; the same notation is used for
(\mathbb{S}O)_{1}
and
(\mathbb{S}O)_{2}
.

Equation (
A
) shows that
{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Delta}^{U}}}\right]
is a linear combination of the target quantity
\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})
, its swapped counterpart
\Tr(\mathbb{S}O\rho^{\otimes 2})
, and several lower-order quantities involving
\rho
and
\rho^{2}
.
Importantly, all of the latter terms, namely

\displaystyle\Tr(\rho^{2}),\ \Tr(O_{1}\rho),\ \Tr(O_{2}\rho),\ \Tr(O_{1}\rho^{2}),\ \Tr(O_{2}\rho^{2}),\ \Tr((\mathbb{S}O)_{1}\rho),\ \Tr((\mathbb{S}O)_{2}\rho),\ \Tr((\mathbb{S}O)_{1}\rho^{2}),\ \Tr((\mathbb{S}O)_{2}\rho^{2}),

(S172)

can be estimated using the standard CBNE protocol (see Theorem
1
in the main text).
Therefore, combining
\hat{\Delta}^{U}
with these CBNE estimators yields an estimation of

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\Big[\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})+\Tr(\mathbb{S}O\rho^{\otimes 2})\Big].

(S173)

In particular, if
\rho
is a pure state, then
\rho^{\otimes 2}\mathbb{S}=\rho^{\otimes 2}
, and (
S173
) reduces exactly to the desired quantity
\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})
.
Moreover, if
O
is an observable supported on the symmetric subspace of
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes 2}
, then

\mathbb{S}O=O
,
and (
S173
) also reduces to
\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})
. Many important observables satisfy this requirement, including
the projector
\Pi_{\rm sym}^{(2)}
onto the symmetric subspace, arbitrary spectral observables of the form

O=\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\phi_{j}}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle\phi_{j}}}\right|
,
where each
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\phi_{j}}}\right\rangle
lies in the symmetric subspace.

In conclusion, the nonlinear function
\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})
can also be estimated within the CBNE framework if
\rho
is known to be pure or
O
is supported on the symmetric subspace.
In these cases, we expect the same concentration mechanism as in the standard CBNE protocol to yield single-setting estimation in the large-dimensional or few-ancilla regimes; a rigorous analysis is left for future work.
Moreover, since the experimental procedure is independent of the observable
O
, the same measurement data can be reused to estimate many different two-body observables simultaneously.

Proof of Eq.
(
A
)
.

The expectation value of
\hat{\Delta}^{U}
(with respect to the randomness in unitary selection and measurement outcomes) reads:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Delta}^{U}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Delta}^{U}\Big|U}}\right]=\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{24\kappa_{4}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\hat{b}_{i_{2}}=b\Big|U}}\right)\cdot\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle\langle b|U}}\right)^{\otimes 2}O}}\right]}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{24\kappa_{4}}{d}\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\,\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{2}\cdot\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle\langle b|U}}\right)^{\otimes 2}O}}\right]}}\right\}=\frac{24\kappa_{4}}{d}\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\,\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2}\otimes O}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle\langle b|U}}\right)^{\otimes 4}}}\right]}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{24\kappa_{4}}{d}\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2}\otimes O}}\right)\Phi_{\rm H}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes{4}}\big)}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{4}}\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes 2}\otimes O}}\right)R_{\pi}}}\right]

\displaystyle=2\Tr(O\rho^{\otimes 2})+2\Tr(\mathbb{S}O\rho^{\otimes 2})+[1+\Tr(\rho^{2})][\Tr(O)+\Tr(\mathbb{S}O)]+2\Tr(O_{1}\rho)+2\Tr(O_{2}\rho)

\displaystyle\quad\ +2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{1}\rho]+2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{2}\rho]+2\Tr(O_{1}\rho^{2})+2\Tr(O_{2}\rho^{2})+2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{1}\rho^{2}]+2\Tr[(\mathbb{S}O)_{2}\rho^{2}],

(S174)

where
(a)
follows from the Haar-twirling identity in Eq. (
S4
).
∎

B
Estimation of multivariate functions

We next discuss the estimation of multivariate nonlinear functions of the form

\displaystyle\Tr(O_{1}\rho_{1}^{t_{1}}O_{2}\rho_{2}^{t_{2}}\cdots O_{m}\rho_{m}^{t_{m}}),

(S175)

where
t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{m}
are positive integers,
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{m}
are possibly distinct quantum states on
\mathcal{H}
, and
O_{1},\dots,O_{m}
are observables on
\mathcal{H}
.
Such quantities arise in tasks such as bounding the quantum Fisher information
Vitale et al. 2024
;
Rath et al. 2021
;
Zhang and Tong 2025
, cross-platform verification
Anshu et al. 2022
;
Elben et al. 2020b
, multivariate trace estimation
Quek et al. 2024
, and many other quantum information-processing applications.

To estimate (
S175
), it suffices to construct an operator-valued estimator of
\rho_{j}^{t_{j}}
for each
j=1,\dots,m
.
This can again be achieved within the CBNE framework.
Suppose copies of a fixed state
\rho
are prepared sequentially.
In each experimental round, we apply a Haar-random unitary
U
to
\rho
, followed by a computational-basis measurement.
Repeating this procedure
N_{M}
times with the same
U
yields a list of measurement outcomes

\mathbf{b}_{U}=\{\hat{b}_{1},\dots,\hat{b}_{N_{M}}\}
.
From these data, we define the estimator

\displaystyle\hat{\Upsilon}_{k}^{U}(\rho):=\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d}\binom{N_{M}}{k}^{-1}\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k}\leq N_{M}}\textbf{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}}}\right\}\cdot U^{{\dagger}}|\hat{b}_{i_{1}}\rangle\langle\hat{b}_{i_{1}}|U.

(S176)

As proved below, its expectation value is

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Upsilon}_{k}^{U}(\rho)}}\right]=\gamma_{k}(\rho):=\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k+1}}\Tr_{1,\dots,k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}\otimes\openone}}\right)R_{\pi}}}\right],

(S177)

where
\Tr_{1,\dots,k}
denotes the partial trace over the first
k
systems.

Note that the operator
\gamma_{k}(\rho)
can be expanded as a polynomial in the moments
p_{i}=\Tr(\rho^{i})

and the state powers
\rho^{j}
, with
2\leq i\leq k
and
0\leq j\leq k
.
For the first few values of
k
, one finds

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{\openone}{2}+\frac{\rho}{2},

\displaystyle\gamma_{2}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+p_{2})\openone}{6}+\frac{\rho}{3}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{3},

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+3p_{2}+2p_{3})\openone}{24}+\frac{(1+p_{2})\rho}{8}+\frac{\rho^{2}}{4}+\frac{\rho^{3}}{4},\quad\dots

(S178)

Therefore, combining the estimators
\hat{\Upsilon}_{i}^{U}(\rho)
with the CBNE estimators for the moments
p_{2},\dots,p_{k}
, one can recursively reconstruct estimators of
\rho,\rho^{2},\dots,\rho^{k}
.
For instance,

\displaystyle\rho

\displaystyle=2\gamma_{1}(\rho)-\openone,

\displaystyle\rho^{2}

\displaystyle=3\gamma_{2}(\rho)-2\gamma_{1}(\rho)+\frac{1-p_{2}}{2}\openone,

\displaystyle\rho^{3}

\displaystyle=4\gamma_{3}(\rho)-3\gamma_{2}(\rho)+(1-p_{2})\gamma_{1}(\rho)+\frac{3p_{2}-1-2p_{3}}{6}\openone,

(S179)

and similarly for higher powers.
We denote by
\widehat{\rho^{k}}
an estimator of
\rho^{k}
constructed in this way.

Applying this construction independently to each state
\rho_{j}
, we obtain estimators

\widehat{\rho_{1}^{t_{1}}},\ \widehat{\rho_{2}^{t_{2}}},\ \dots,\ \widehat{\rho_{m}^{t_{m}}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\Tr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(O_{1}\widehat{\rho_{1}^{t_{1}}}O_{2}\widehat{\rho_{2}^{t_{2}}}\cdots O_{m}\widehat{\rho_{m}^{t_{m}}}}}\right)

(S180)

can be used to approximate the target quantity (
S175
), provided that the random data used to construct the estimators
\widehat{\rho_{j}^{t_{j}}}
are independent across different
j
.

We remark that this independence requirement means that the random unitaries used for different states
\rho_{j}
should be chosen independently.
Therefore, unlike the single-state quantities considered before, one should not expect a single measurement setting to suffice in general when
m\geq 2
.
Nevertheless, the total number of required settings may still remain very small: we expect that
m
independent settings, one for each state
\rho_{j}
, should be sufficient in the large-dimensional regime or with the aid of a few ancillary qubits.
We leave a rigorous performance analysis for future work.
Finally, we emphasize again that, since the experimental protocol for each
\rho_{j}
is independent of
O_{1},\dots,O_{m}
, the same measurement data can be reused to estimate many different multivariate functions simultaneously.

Proof of Eq.
(
S177
)
.

The expectation of
\hat{\Upsilon}_{k}^{U}(\rho)
(with respect to the randomness in unitary selection and measurement outcomes) reads:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Upsilon}_{k}^{U}(\rho)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\,\underset{\,\mathbf{b}_{U}}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\hat{\Upsilon}_{k}^{U}(\rho)\Big|U}}\right]=\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Pr\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\hat{b}_{i_{1}}=\dots=\hat{b}_{i_{k}}=b\,|U}}\right)\cdot U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle\langle b|U}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d}\,\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\,\underset{U}{{\mathbb{E}}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\cdot U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle\langle b|U}}\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{\kappa_{k+1}}{d}\sum_{b=0}^{d-1}\Tr_{1,\dots,k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}\otimes\openone}}\right)\Phi_{\rm H}\big(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|^{\otimes{(k+1)}}\big)}}\right]

\displaystyle\,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathrm{S}_{k+1}}\Tr_{1,\dots,k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}\otimes\openone}}\right)R_{\pi}}}\right]=\gamma_{k}(\rho),

(S181)

where
(a)
holds because
\langle b|U\rho U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle^{k}\cdot U^{{\dagger}}|b\rangle\langle b|U=\Tr_{1,\dots,k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\otimes k}\otimes\openone}}\right)\big(U^{{\dagger}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|b}}\right\rangle\!\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle b}}\right|U\big)^{\otimes(k+1)}}}\right]
, and
(b)
follows from Eq. (
S4
).
∎

S9
More numerical results

In this section, we provide additional numerical results supporting the performance of CBNE and PTME.
Throughout this paper, the random unitaries used in the simulations are generated by finite-depth two-local brickwork circuits rather than exact global unitary designs.
Specifically, a brickwork circuit consists of alternating layers of nearest-neighbor two-qubit gates: odd layers act on qubit pairs
(1,2),(3,4),\ldots
, while even layers act on pairs
(2,3),(4,5),\ldots
.
Each two-qubit gate is independently sampled from the Haar measure.
Such local random circuits are known to form approximate unitary designs

Brandão et al. 2016
;
Haferkamp 2022
.
Thus, our numerical results also serve as a practical check that the predicted performance of CBNE and PTME persists when exact unitary designs are replaced by experimentally more feasible approximate designs, consistent with the theoretical guarantees in the End Matter.

A
Sample-complexity scaling with system size

In this subsection, we provide additional numerical evidence for the sample-complexity scalings predicted by Theorems
1
and
3
in the main text.
We focus on third-order quantities, since
t=3
already captures the nonlinear scaling behavior of our estimators.

We first consider the estimation of the state moment
p_{3}=\Tr(\rho^{3})
using the CBNE protocol.
According to Theorem
1
, the total number of measurements required to estimate
p_{3}
up to a constant additive error
\epsilon
scales as

N_{\mathrm{tot}}^{\mathrm{CBNE}}=N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d^{2/3}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2^{\frac{2}{3}n}}}\right),

(S182)

where
n
is the number of qubits in
\rho
and
d=2^{n}
is the system dimension.

To demonstrate this prediction, we use the same noisy TFIM ground states as in the main text,

\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+p\mathbb{I}/d
,
with
J=h=1
and
p=0.2
.
For each system size
n
, we fix the number of random unitaries to
N_{U}=200
, and then determine the smallest number of measurements per unitary
N_{M}
such that the statistical error falls below the threshold
0.1
.
Here
N_{U}
is chosen to be larger than one only to suppress statistical fluctuations and make the scaling with
n
more clearly visible in the numerics.
This choice is made only for numerical stability and visualization; it does not contradict our theoretical result that, when the system dimension is sufficiently large, single-setting estimation with
N_{U}=1
is already sufficient.
Our numerical results, shown in Fig.
S1
(a), indicate that the fitted scaling of the total sample complexity
N_{U}N_{M}
is consistent with the theoretical prediction

N_{\mathrm{tot}}^{\mathrm{CBNE}}\propto 2^{2n/3}
.

Fig. S1:
Scaling of the minimal number of measurements
N_{U}N_{M}
required to estimate (a) the state moment
\Tr(\rho^{3})
and (b) the PT moment
\Tr\!\big[(\rho_{AB}^{\top_{\!B}})^{3}\big]
up to a fixed statistical error of 0.1, for fixed
N_{U}=200
.
Here
\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+pI/d
, where
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
is the ground state of
n
-qubit TFIM with
J=h=1.0
and depolarizing probability
p=0.2
.
For each data point, we choose the minimal
N_{M}
such that the statistical error
\leq 0.1
averaged over
50
experiments.
In panel (b), the total system has
n=n_{A}+1
qubits; subsystem
A
consists of the first
n_{A}
qubits, and subsystem
B
is the last qubit.

We next examine the sample complexity of our PTME protocol.
For estimating the PT moment
p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}=\Tr\!\big[(\rho_{AB}^{\top_{\!B}})^{3}\big]
, Theorem
3
in the main text gives the required total number of measurements

N_{\mathrm{tot}}^{\mathrm{PTME}}=N_{U}N_{M}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{d_{B}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{d_{A}d_{B}^{2}}{\epsilon}}}\right)^{2/3}}}\right\}}}\right].

(S183)

When the subsystem
B
and the target accuracy
\epsilon
are fixed, this becomes

N_{\mathrm{tot}}^{\mathrm{PTME}}=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(d_{A}^{2/3}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2^{\frac{2}{3}n_{A}}}}\right),

(S184)

where
n_{A}
and
n_{B}
denote the qubit number of subsystems
A
and
B
, respectively.
Figure
S1
(b) verifies this theoretical prediction numerically, in which we fix
N_{U}=200
,
n_{B}=1
, and choose, for each
n_{A}
, the smallest number
N_{M}
required to reach the same target statistical error 0.1.
The fitted exponent is close to the expected value
2/3
, supporting the sample-complexity scaling in Eq. (
S184
).

Together, Fig.
S1
(a,b) confirms that both CBNE and PTME exhibit the predicted sample-complexity scaling that is sublinear in the system dimension for estimating nonlinear moment quantities, which is consistent with our theoretical results.

B
Exponential error suppression in CBNE and PTME

In this subsection, we provide additional numerical evidence for the dimension-dependent error suppression predicted by our theory.
Recall that, for CBNE, Theorem
1
shows that the number of measurement settings required to estimate
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
is

N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\,c\mathfrak{B}/(d\epsilon^{2})}}\right\}
,
where
c
is a constant factor,
d
is the system dimension, and
\mathfrak{B}=\max\{\Tr(O_{0}^{2}),1\}
.
Thus, increasing the dimension
d
reduces the required setting number and eventually drives the protocol into the single-setting regime.
Equivalently, when
N_{U}=1
is fixed, the statistical estimation error is expected to decrease as the effective dimension increases.
This is the mechanism behind the ancilla-assisted version of CBNE: appending
n_{a}
ancillary qubits increases the effective dimension from
d
to
2^{n_{a}}d
, while preserving the target value
\Tr(O\rho^{t})
.

Fig. S2:
Average statistical error in estimating
\Tr(O\rho^{3})
via CBNE.
Here
\rho
is the Gibbs state of the 6-qubit 1D TFIM with
J=h=1.0
and
\beta=2.0
.
The observables are Pauli operators
O=X_{1}X_{2}\cdots X_{6}
,
O=Z_{1}Z_{2}
, and the ground state of the TFIM
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|
.
The upper plots show the error scaling with
N_{M}
for
N_{U}=1
and different numbers of ancillary qubits
n_{a}
.
The lower plots show the error scaling with
n_{a}
for fixed
N_{M}=10^{6}
and different values of
N_{U}
.

Fig. S3:
Average statistical error in estimating
p_{4}^{\mathrm{PT}}
via PTME. Here
\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+pI/d
, with
p=0.3
, and
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
is the ground state of the 1D TFIM with
J=h=1.0
.
The size of subsystem
B
is fixed at
n_{B}=2
.
(a) Error scaling versus
N_{M}
for
N_{U}=1
and different subsystem sizes
n_{A}
.
(b) Error scaling versus
n_{A}
for fixed
N_{M}=10^{7}
and different
N_{U}
.

We first test this prediction for CBNE.
The target state
\rho
is chosen as a Gibbs state of the six-qubit 1D TFIM, and we estimate nonlinear quantities
\Tr(O\rho^{3})
for three observables:

X_{1}X_{2}X_{3}X_{4}X_{5}X_{6},\qquad Z_{1}Z_{2}I_{3}I_{4}I_{5}I_{6},\qquad|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|,

where
X_{i}
and
Z_{i}
denote the Pauli-
X
and Pauli-
Z
operators acting on the
i
-th qubit,
and
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
denotes the TFIM ground state.
For each value of
n_{a}
, we append
n_{a}
ancillary qubits initialized in the fixed pure state
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|0^{n_{a}}}}\right\rangle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\langle 0^{n_{a}}}}\right|
and run CBNE with a single measurement setting,
N_{U}=1
.
As shown in Fig.
S2
, the statistical error decreases exponentially with
n_{a}
.
This agrees with the theoretical prediction above, confirming that enlarging the effective Hilbert space through ancillary qubits enables high-precision estimation with a single measurement setting.
Moreover, the estimates for all three observables in Fig.
S2
are obtained by postprocessing the same measurement data, demonstrating that CBNE can simultaneously estimate multiple distinct observables.

We next consider PTME, for which
Theorem
3
in the main text predicts a similar dimension-dependent suppression.
For estimating PT moments, the number of measurement settings scales as
N_{U}=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\,c\,d_{B}/(d_{A}\epsilon^{2})}}\right\}
,
where
d_{A}
and
d_{B}
are the dimensions of subsystems
A
and
B
, respectively.
Hence, for fixed
d_{B}
, increasing
d_{A}
or adding ancillary qubits to subsystem
A
reduces the setting complexity and suppresses the single-setting statistical error.
We verify this behavior numerically using depolarized TFIM ground states.
We fix subsystem
B
to contain
n_{B}=2
qubits and vary the size
n_{A}
of subsystem
A
.
As shown in Fig.
S3
, the statistical error for estimating the fourth order PT moment
p_{4}^{\mathrm{PT}}
decreases exponentially as
n_{A}
increases, confirming the error suppression predicted by our theory.

C
PTME for entanglement detection

In this subsection, we demonstrate how PTME can be used for entanglement detection through PT-moment witnesses.

Entanglement is one of the central resources in quantum information science
Horodecki et al. 2009
. It enables the advantage of quantum communication over classical strategies. Therefore, entanglement detection is a fundamental problem in quantum resource theory. Among the various criteria developed, the Positive Partial Transposition (PPT) criterion stands out as one of the most important entanglement detection criteria
Peres 1996
. Suppose an
n
-qubit state
\rho
on Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{B}
. The PPT criterion states that for all separable states,
\rho^{\top_{B}}
remains positive, while for entangled states,
\rho^{\top_{B}}
may not be.
Thus, the positivity of
\rho^{\top_{B}}
can be used as a criterion for detecting entanglement. It has been shown that, for all pure states and low-dimensional mixed states (
2\times 2
and
2\times 3
), PPT provides a necessary and sufficient condition for separability
Horodecki et al. 1996
.

Although the PPT criterion provides a clear and straightforward method for entanglement detection, determining whether
\rho^{\top_{B}}
is positive requires precise estimation of all its eigenvalues, which is challenging in practical scenarios.
As a result, a series of entanglement criteria based on partial transpose (PT) moments have emerged.
While these criteria are generally weaker than the PPT criterion, they offer a simpler implementation and can be extracted through techniques such as randomized measurements
Elben et al. 2020a
;
Zhou et al. 2020
;
Vermersch et al. 2024a
;
Gray et al. 2018
;
Carteret 2005
;
Cornfeld et al. 2019
.

A class of PT-moment criteria is based on the Stieltjes moment problem
Yu et al. 2021
.
For
k\geq 0
, define the
(k+1)\times(k+1)
Hankel matrix

\big[B_{k}(\rho^{\top_{B}})\big]_{i,j}=p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{i+j+1},\qquad i,j=0,\ldots,k.

(S185)

If
\rho
is separable, then the moment sequence
\{p_{t}^{\mathrm{PT}}\}_{t=1}^{m}
must satisfy

B_{\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor}(\rho^{\top_{B}})\geq 0
.
A weaker but simpler necessary condition is

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|B_{\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor}(\rho^{\top_{B}})}}\right|:=\det\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[B_{\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho^{\top_{B}}}}\right)}}\right]\geq 0.

(S186)

Since
B_{k}
is the leading principal submatrix of
B_{k+1}
, the condition
B_{k+1}\geq 0
gives a stronger entanglement criterion than
B_{k}\geq 0
.
For
k=1
, using
p_{1}^{\mathrm{PT}}=1
, the positivity condition
B_{1}(\rho^{\top_{B}})\geq 0
implies

p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}-(p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}})^{2}\geq 0
.
Therefore, a violation,

(p_{2}^{\mathrm{PT}})^{2}-p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}>0
,
certifies entanglement; this is the well-known
p_{3}^{\mathrm{PT}}
-PPT criterion
Elben et al. 2020a
.

We next present a numerical experiment in which our PTME protocol is used to estimate PT moments and construct the Hankel-matrix witnesses for entanglement detection.
The states considered here are Gibbs states of the TFIM.
At low temperature, i.e., large
\beta
, the Gibbs state approaches the TFIM ground state and is typically entangled, whereas at high temperature, i.e., small
\beta
, it approaches the maximally mixed state and becomes separable.
As shown in Fig.
S4
, the Hankel criterion based on
B_{1}(\rho^{\top_{B}})
fails to detect entanglement for certain intermediate values of
\beta
.
In contrast, the higher-order criterion based on
B_{2}(\rho^{\top_{B}})
, which uses PT moments up to order five, can detect entanglement in this regime.
This illustrates the advantage of accessing higher-order PT moments.
At the same time, higher-order criteria are more sensitive to statistical noise, since they involve higher-order moments and higher-degree polynomial functions of the estimated moments.
PTME is useful in this setting because the same measurement data can be reused to estimate all required PT moments, allowing one to combine lower-order and higher-order criteria to improve the overall detection performance.

Fig. S4:
Values of the entanglement-detection criteria constructed from
B_{k}(\rho^{\top_{B}})
as functions of the inverse temperature
\beta
, where positive values detect entanglement.
In the plot, the matrix
B_{k}(\rho^{\top_{B}})
is abbreviated as
B_{k}
, and
|B_{k}|:=\det(B_{k})
.
Here
\rho
is a 12-qubit Gibbs state of the TFIM with
J=1
and
h=2.5
.
Subsystem
A
consists of the first 11 qubits, and subsystem
B
is the remaining one qubit.
Each point is obtained by averaging the PTME estimates over 1000 independent trials with
N_{U}=100
and
N_{M}=10^{6}
.
The shaded regions is plotted according to the standard deviation.

Another class of PT-moment criteria is given by the
D_{k}
witness hierarchy
Neven et al. 2021
.
Let
e_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})
denote the
k
-th elementary symmetric polynomial,

e_{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}):=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{k}\leq d}x_{j_{1}}\cdots x_{j_{k}},\qquad k=1,\ldots,d,\qquad e_{0}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})=1.

(S187)

Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d}
be the eigenvalues of
\rho^{\top_{B}}
.
The criterion states that, if
\rho
is separable, then

e_{k}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})\geq 0

for all
k=1,\ldots,d
.
Therefore, a negative value of
e_{k}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
indicates the existence of entanglement.
Using Newton’s identities, each
e_{k}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d})
can be expressed as a polynomial in the first
k
PT moments
p_{1}^{\mathrm{PT}},\ldots,p_{k}^{\mathrm{PT}}
.
Define

D_{k}:=-k\,e_{k}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{d}),

so that
D_{k}>0
detects entanglement
Neven et al. 2021
.
The first few polynomials are

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=-p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1},

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{2}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle=-p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{3}+\frac{3}{2}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{2}-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{3},

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle=p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{4}-\frac{1}{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{2}-p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{2}}}\right]^{2}+\frac{1}{3}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{4}-\frac{4}{3}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{3},

\displaystyle D_{5}

\displaystyle=-p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{5}+\frac{5}{4}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{4}+\frac{5}{6}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{2}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{3}-\frac{5}{6}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{2}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{3}-\frac{5}{8}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{2}}}\right)^{2}+\frac{5}{12}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{3}p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{2}-\frac{1}{24}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(p^{\mathrm{PT}}_{1}}}\right)^{5}.

(S188)

Fig. S5:
Values of the
D_{k}
entanglement witnesses estimated by PTME as functions of the depolarizing probability
p
, where positive values detect entanglement.
Here
\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+pI/d
, and
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
is the ground state of the 12-qubit Heisenberg model with
J=1
.
Subsystem
A
consists of the first 11 qubits, and subsystem
B
is the remaining qubit.
Each point is obtained by averaging over 1000 independent PTME trials with
N_{U}=1
and
N_{M}=10^{6}
.
The shaded regions indicate the standard error.

Next, we demonstrate the use of PTME for entanglement detection with the
D_{k}
witness hierarchy.
We consider the depolarized state

\rho=(1-p)|\mathrm{gs}\rangle\langle\mathrm{gs}|+pI/d
,
where
|\mathrm{gs}\rangle
is the ground state of the one-dimensional Heisenberg model with open boundary conditions, whose Hamiltonian is written as

H=-J\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(X_{i}X_{i+1}+Y_{i}Y_{i+1}+Z_{i}Z_{i+1}}}\right).

(S189)

Figure
S5
shows that higher-order witnesses can detect entanglement in certain regimes where lower-order witnesses fail.
PTME is useful in this setting because it estimates the required PT moments simultaneously from a single measurement setting, allowing one to combine
D_{k}
witnesses of different orders and thereby detect a broader range of entangled states.
